13 Black Women Share How They Found the Strength to Survive—and Thrive—With Breast Cancer

📅 2021-10-18  |  Read Source →

When it comes to Black women’s breast health, the statistics rarely tell a hopeful story. Black women are diagnosed with breast cancer at a younger median age (60, compared with 62 for white women) and are 38% more likely to die from the disease. They have the lowest survival rate for every known stage of breast cancer. And about one in five Black women with breast cancer are diagnosed with hard-to-treat triple-negative breast cancer (TNBC), more than any other racial or ethnic group.

But here’s what the numbers will never capture: the phone calls at 3 a.m., the head-shaving parties, the therapy sessions that cracked something open, the moment a woman looks at her daughter or granddaughter and decides they are reason enough to fight. Black women have always been more than a data point, and perhaps nowhere is that truer than in the way they face, survive, and remake their lives after a breast cancer diagnosis.

The stories below belong to 13 Black women who refused to be reduced to a statistic. They are individuals with careers, children, humor, faith, rage, and joy whose experiences with breast cancer changed them in ways both devastating and profound. In finding their own paths through treatment and beyond, they’ve built something the data can’t quantify: community, purpose, and a hard-won blueprint for survival they’re passing on to every woman who comes after them.

Monique Bass, 53, had always been proactive about her health. A single mother of twin sons and grandmother of four, she started getting annual mammograms at 40. But it was a self-exam at 47 while lying in bed one night that changed everything. She found a lump that didn’t feel right.

What followed were three tissue biopsies of the lump over three years, each coming back benign. Doctors wanted to keep monitoring. Bass pushed back. The lump was growing, and she knew her body well enough to realize something was wrong. She insisted they remove the lump (a procedure called a lumpectomy) and test it. The diagnosis: stage 1 invasive ductal carcinoma that was aggressive enough to require immediate chemotherapy.

Bass got the call while sitting in a car wash. “I felt like I was in The Twilight Zone,” she says. “Not in a million years did I think I would ever hear the words, ‘You have cancer.’” The timing made everything harder. It was 2020, the height of the pandemic, so she navigated much of her journey alone. Adding “another emotional layer,” she was also going through a divorce. Treatment included four rounds of doxorubicin, four rounds of paclitaxel, and 21 radiation treatments. Losing her hair hit the hardest. “But the silver lining is that it grew back even more beautifully after treatment,” Bass says.

Throughout it all her faith anchored her. “I’m not a deeply religious person, but I believe in God, and I knew he had me in the palm of his hand,” Bass says. On the other side of treatment, she founded What’s Behind the Bra?, a New Jersey nonprofit that distributes comfort care packages to breast cancer patients year-round and hosts a weekly podcast of the same name every Sunday at 7:30 p.m. ET.

Jaqueline Beale, 64, comes from a family with a long history of cancer, including her mother, who had breast cancer. That’s why it wasn’t a huge surprise when, at 40, she found a lump during a breast self-exam. Even though a sonogram and mammogram came back clear, it was obvious that something wasn’t right, so Beale got a biopsy.

When the radiologist called with her result—stage 1 breast cancer, which means the cancer hadn’t spread beyond where the abnormal cells had developed—she could hear a lot of noise in the background. He said, “I’m in New York trying to hail a cab, but you have breast cancer. You need to find a breast surgeon.” Despite the bluntness, Beale says she couldn’t help but find the humor in the situation, which is something she carried with her through treatment.

“I made sure that I surrounded myself with laughter and love,” she says, “and I told my family that I knew they loved me, but I also needed a lot of laughter to get through it.”

She recalls one incident in an elevator after her sister had taken her to a chemo treatment. “I had been sick as a dog after, and a woman got in and asked if I was okay. My sister said, ‘Oh, she’s fine. She’s just got a little bit of cancer.’ The woman looked horrified, but I laughed. My family knew that their sense of humor is what I needed.”

Annita White, 51, was five months late for her mammogram. As a single mother of two boys, a higher education administrator, and a first-year PhD student, she always had something more pressing. When she finally went, she expected the usual letter in the mail saying everything was fine. Instead, she got a call. There was a mass that hadn’t been there before.

By the time the biopsy came back, she already knew. A technician had commented on how hard the mass was, and White felt the diagnosis in her spirit before the words were ever spoken. The confirmation came the next day. “When I got the call, I fell to the floor and cried tears of desperation,” she says. “My first thought was, ‘Lord, I can’t do this.’”

What followed was hard in every way: chemotherapy, losing her breasts, losing her hair, which had always been full. “Being bald left me feeling vulnerable and insecure,” White says. “Physically, losing my breasts and going through chemotherapy made me feel like my body was no longer my own.” Through it all the fear that drove her was running out of time—time to see her sons become men and to meet her grandchildren someday.

Her faith, which she’d been privately wrestling with for years, met her exactly where she was. She’d been asking God to truly show up in her life. “He did,” she says. “In the middle of one of the hardest seasons of my life, he carried me through.”

Three years later White is cancer-free, her hair is back, and she recently finished her PhD. She now walks in her purpose as Dr. Nita, a member of the African American Breast Cancer Alliance survivor network, sharing her story and those of others facing the disease. “What once felt like the end became the beginning of something greater,” she says.

Marylande Regis, 38, had been in her new Georgia home for four months when everything changed. A registered nurse, realtor, wife, and mother of three, she had just stopped breastfeeding her nine-month-old when she found the lump. Her baby had never latched well on that side. When she messaged her provider, the on-call response was that it was probably a clogged milk duct and that she was too young to worry about it being something more serious. Regis didn’t accept that. She called back and left a message, insisting that something was wrong. “Another ob-gyn saw the message and encouraged me to make an office appointment immediately,” she says. Within two weeks she had a diagnosis: aggressive stage 2B, BRCA1-positive breast cancer. Within two more weeks what had been one tumor and one impacted lymph node had become three tumors and four impacted lymph nodes. Regis was 36.

“I was relieved that I advocated for myself,” she says. “I was proud that fear didn’t factor into my decision.”

What followed was relentless. Regis went through six months of chemotherapy, a bilateral mastectomy, six weeks of radiation, a total hysterectomy (to eliminate her body’s main source of estrogen), and breast reconstruction, all over a span of roughly 18 months. She lost friends to cancer along the way, as well as a cousin diagnosed around the same time. Her faith was, as Regis describes it, “shaken and stirred and flipped every way possible.” She wrote the important letters. She wrote her own obituary. She was practical about it. “I wanted to be positive, but I am also very realistic,” Regis says.

The hardest part wasn’t any single treatment—it was losing control. As her family’s go-to planner for everything from doctor’s appointments to vacations, she found it foreign to ask for help. But when she told her parents, her mother showed up and never left. “We joke, but my mom never went home,” Regis says. “This is her house now.” Her husband, Kory, came to every appointment and held steady even through both of them losing their jobs. “He deserves a trophy,” she says.

As the daughter of Haitian immigrants, raised to be everything her parents couldn’t be, Regis says the fight was never just medical. Today strength looks different than it did before cancer. It looks like making it to the bus stop with her son, who has autism. It looks like seeing her oldest finish nursing school and work toward becoming an ob-gyn. It looks like watching her youngest hit milestones like learning to talk. “I would have missed that if I didn’t fight,” she says. “The clouds are moving. The sun has decided to show itself. I can’t wait to soak it all in.”

Illness had always been an afterthought for 75-year-old Judy Fambrough-Billingsley. Why would it cross her mind? She walked three miles every day, swam laps all summer, took Zumba classes, and played soccer for 30 years. Judy Fambrough-Billingsley had never smoked or drank, and she didn’t even need to take medication. Being healthy wasn’t something she worked at; it was just who she was. A naturalized American citizen born in Germany, she traveled back to her home country regularly to visit family. Her life was full and active.

So when she felt a lump—and then another six months later—she ignored them. After all, she’d had a clean mammogram earlier that year. But when she noticed them growing and multiplying, she went back to her doctor. The results were not what she expected.

“I was shocked,” Fambrough-Billingsley says. She suddenly found herself navigating an entirely new set of experiences: prescription bottles, hospital procedures, lab processes—the whole medical system. “Being a part of a world where sickness exists had never been my world until now,” she says.

What carried her through was far older than her diagnosis. She grew up during the Civil Rights Era, the daughter of a successful Black entrepreneur who built his business during tumultuous years in US history. Both her parents were lifetime members of the National Association for the Advancement of Colored People (NAACP). She was president of the NAACP Youth Council and an active member of the Student Nonviolent Coordinating Committee. Fambrough-Billingsley knows something about fighting for your life in systems that weren’t built for you.

“Growing up during the Civil Rights Era contributed to this era of fighting,” she says. “Never giving up.” That same spirit—the one her parents modeled, the one she fell into during her work as a community organizer and across decades of a life fully lived—is the one she’s bringing to this too. She may still be on her breast cancer journey, but this is not her first fight.

Taylor Johnson was nearly 30 when she found the lump on her left breast. A mom and content creator who loves trying new things and staying active, she went to her primary care doctor, who referred her for an ultrasound and a mammogram. Johnson was denied a mammogram—she was told she was too young. She pushed anyway, got a biopsy (in lieu of the mammogram), and days later, had a diagnosis: stage 2 triple-negative breast cancer.

“My reaction was a very deep pause, trying to process what my doctor had told me,” Johnson says. “It then hit me, and all I could do was cry.”

She’s in the middle of her treatment plan, which includes six months of chemotherapy, followed by surgery and three weeks of radiation. After finishing chemo Johnson felt confident that would be the hardest part of her journey, both physically and emotionally. But her own health wasn’t her only concern: She knew her five-year-old was watching her every move, and she wanted to stay stable for her daughter. “I was so used to always being the strong one,” Johnson says. “This definitely tested that.” She held it together not because it came easily, but because she understood what her daughter was absorbing.

She is grateful for the community that rallied around her while she waited. Weekly prayer calls, rotating visits from family and friends, and a medical team at Mayo Clinic in Arizona that went above and beyond carried her through.

Though Johnson hasn’t finished treatment yet, completing chemo clarified something for her. “It has reassured me how strong I truly am,” she says. Strength, she’s learned, isn’t just physical. The emotional and mental muscle it takes to keep going counts just as much.

Jessica Ncube, 41, built her life around caring for others. A licensed clinical social worker, the co-owner of a mental health practice, and a mom, she was always in motion. Her life revolved around showing up for others and helping them navigate their hardest moments. “Looking back I do not know that I was always receiving,” she says.

Her diagnosis came quietly. A routine mammogram caught it; nothing in her body had felt amiss. That silence, Ncube says, is exactly why early detection matters. When she got the news she went into survival mode. “You hear the words, but it takes time for them to land,” she says.

Ncube’s journey started with a lumpectomy. When her margins came back unclear, meaning the pathologist could not determine whether all the cancerous cells were removed, she made the decision to move forward with a double mastectomy with reconstructive surgery. “Losing a part of your body, even when it is lifesaving, is still a loss,” she says. “The hardest part was learning how to sit in both gratitude and grief at the same time.”

As someone professionally wired to be strong for others, letting that tendency go was its own kind of work. “Strength became less about holding everything in and more about being honest about what I needed,” Ncube says. Her faith grounded her, and her community showed up, but she also had to unlearn something deeply familiar. “As a Black woman there is a resilience that is almost ingrained in us,” she says. “I had to learn how to balance that resilience with softness.”

After completing treatment Ncube has become more present and deliberate. Her definition of strength has changed entirely. “Strength today is softness. It is honesty. It is allowing myself to receive, to rest, and to not have all the answers,” she says.

Ameiko Newman, 40, didn’t learn of her diagnosis through a routine screening. She was rushed to the hospital for abdominal pain, and that’s when she learned she had breast cancer. Life before her diagnosis had been stressful, uncertain, a season of not quite knowing where she was headed. In a strange way, cancer gave her direction.

“For me, cancer saved me,” she says. “It made me a better person. It made me fight harder, push through the hard times and know what I want in life.”

Newman is still in treatment, receiving chemotherapy and immunotherapy every week, and she is honest about what that looks like: There are good days and bad days. She goes to church faithfully, prays every day, and tries to stay positive through it all.

What has surprised her most is that in the middle of fighting for her own life, she found her purpose: helping others fight for theirs. Newman rebranded her business entirely around that mission and started designing scarves with chemo patients in mind. “I started making them mainly for my Black women because you know we love having style,” she says. But a wide variety of women from all over have since bought them.

She wakes up every day, gets her kids ready, works out, and moves through life. Faith, she says, is what makes that possible. Her message to any Black woman who’s just gotten diagnosed is both simple and hard-won: “Cancer doesn’t define you. You define you. Even though you’re fighting for your life, help someone else fight for theirs.”

Charlotte Connor, 38, had just undergone a breast augmentation when she felt a lump in her breast. “When I saw my doctor, they weren’t concerned, in part because of my young age,” says Connor, who was 30 years old at the time. She had a mammogram and ultrasound to check for signs of cancer, but no MRI. “Looking back now I wish I would have asked for that MRI,” she says.

At the first checkup six months later, they told her she was fine. At the second—one year after the original lump was found—the lump had tripled in size. Her doctor ordered a biopsy, and her diagnosis was confirmed: stage 2A ductal carcinoma, which means the cancer was growing but contained to the breast and surrounding lymph nodes.

“When they told me I had cancer, I was calm,” she says. Her mindset immediately shifted from a sinking feeling to being optimistic about the future. “I focused on getting healthy again,” says Connor, who started fantasizing about what she would do after treatment.

She remembers thinking, I may be feeling sick and throwing up from chemotherapy, but what restaurants am I going to go to after all this is over? It’s an outlook that didn’t necessarily come naturally to her, either. “I’m not even overly optimistic in life—I think I’m a realist—but in this situation, I shifted to being overly optimistic.”

Part of that mindset shift was for her young daughter. “I couldn’t be pessimistic; I had to be strong for her,” she says. “And I knew that how I acted in this situation was how she was going to mirror things and react to stressful situations when she got older.”

Connor’s other pillar of support came from a tight-knit circle of friends and family, who threw her a head-shaving party before she lost her hair due to chemo. “Making things fun and lighthearted like that got me through my darkest, saddest moments,” she says.

It was 1999, and Donna Culmer had just received a clean mammogram. A few months later she felt a stone-size bump on her right side, so she went back to her ob-gyn to get it checked out. They redid the mammogram on both breasts, and while what she’d felt was actually a fibrocystic lump, the imaging found a small, not palpable spot on her left side that turned out to be a stage 1 cancer. “I was stunned,” Culmer says. “I didn’t even know what questions to ask.” She drove back to work in a daze and told her boss what had happened.

At 46 she was serving in the Navy as an instructor and trainer to personnel who were working alongside the Marines. She was married to a Marine and raising three children. There was no good moment for this. There never is.

Four months of chemotherapy followed, with nausea and exhaustion that never quite let up. Her doctors were concerned that the chemo might not work, so she had a mastectomy. Through it all her community refused to let her go through it alone. Her father drove her to every session and sat with her. Her church came to her, literally; services were held at her house.

Twenty-seven years later, Culmer, now 73, has had time to reflect. “As Black women, we have been through challenges our whole lives,” she says. “This is just one more.” Her advice is practical and clear. “Don’t panic and ask questions, even if you have to write them down,” Culmer says. “The treatments available now are so much better than what I had.”

One morning, Patricia Fox, 38, woke up with a lump in her right breast that wasn’t there the night before—she was just 26 years old at the time. Fox scheduled an appointment with her ob-gyn that day. “They told me that I was a young Black woman and had dense breasts, and it was likely a cyst,” she says. “They were ready to turn me away.”

The lump, which was a little smaller than a golf ball, seemed strange to Fox. Thankfully, she listened to her intuition. “There was something telling me to get it checked out,” she says, so her doctor did a biopsy just to be sure.

Her results came back as estrogen receptor-positive stage 2A breast cancer, meaning her cancer had estrogen receptors that would respond to hormone therapy drugs. Fox says she’ll never forget her doctor’s words: “Patricia, you’ll be a breast cancer survivor.” That sentiment set the tone for her mindset as a fighter. “It made me think that I could face this and survive this,” she says.

Still, cancer treatments quickly overwhelmed Fox, and soon she faced her lowest point: crying on the floor for hours, feeling like having cancer at such a young age was a punishment for being a “bad person.” But she pulled herself out of it with hard work in therapy.

“I found that talking to a therapist helped me process my diagnosis and examine the areas of my life—certain people and my own behavior patterns—that were cancerous for me that I needed to do something about,” she says. “While therapy didn’t make the pain go away, it did make how I coped with it much better.”

In the Black community, therapy is often taboo, says Fox, but it’s become her mission to dispel that belief. “Therapy fortified me to get through cancer,” she says, “and I tell everyone that they don’t have to suffer in silence.”

Annette Colden, 63, was sitting at her sister’s bedside during the last days of her final battle with breast cancer when the conversation turned to screenings. Her sister had been through cervical cancer and two bouts of breast cancer before passing away from the disease. “She made me promise I’d get my mammograms,” Colden says.

Colden’s first two yearly mammograms were clear. On the third there was a spot, but she was told by her doctor that things looked okay and it wasn’t cancer—she just had lumpy breasts.

“There’s this inner feeling you have,” she says. “Something was inside me saying, That’s not right.” Her sister’s doctor had told her the exact same thing, but Colden was so happy to hear she didn’t have cancer that she didn’t follow up.

A year later she went back for a mammogram, and that’s when her doctors found it: stage 0 breast cancer, meaning only abnormal cells had been found. She was 42. “Now, looking back, I always tell women that if something doesn’t feel right, get a second opinion.”

Despite the diagnosis Colden remained calm. “I’m a religious person,” she says. “In my prayer I said, ‘Lord, if you will be my pilot on this journey, I’ll be your copilot.’” She immediately felt a sense of peace.

After her recovery she decided to share that calming presence with other women going through breast cancer at a support group through Roswell Park Comprehensive Cancer Center, where she had treatment. “I say, ‘It’s going to be a long road. If you don’t want to travel it alone, I’m right there with you.’”

And she means it. Sometimes that involves talking with someone at 3 a.m. when the pain from chemotherapy hits. Other times it means sending food delivery boxes to the homes of people with breast cancer who can’t muster the strength to leave. “My sister did a lot of support work before she got really sick,” she says. “This is her vision.”

Ricki Fairley, 69, was in the security line at the airport for a work trip when she got a call from her doctor. They’d found a peanut-like lump under her nipple that turned out to be cancer.

Fairley, who was 55 at the time, said, “I don’t have time for this right now. I’ll call you when I get to my destination.” A couple days later she found out she had stage 3A TNBC, and it was really aggressive. Compared with other breast cancers, TNBC has fewer targeted-therapy treatment options, often spreads beyond the breasts, and is more likely to recur. Black women have nearly three times the risk of developing triple-negative breast cancer.

Her diagnosis woke her up. “It made me realize that I needed to get all of the ‘cancers’ out of my life,” Fairley says. During treatment that first year which involved a double mastectomy, aggressive chemotherapy, and radiation, she quit her job and started her own company. Then she filed for divorce and later sold her house. “I quit my life and started a new one, and changed everything,” she says. “I had to learn that my peace is nonnegotiable. I really think that stress caused my breast cancer.”

A year later her life changed again. Fairley was diagnosed with metastatic breast cancer and told she had two years to live. After finding out her current doctor had only treated two previous cases of TNBC and both women had died in eight months, she found a new doctor, who was well-versed in current research and treatments for the disease. She beat cancer a second time. “I remember sitting at my daughter’s graduation thinking, Okay, I made it. What’s next for me?” Fairley says. The answer: helping others. In the years that followed, Fairley cofounded The Black Breast Cancer Alliance, which works to reduce the mortality rate for Black women.

9 Best Desks for Small Spaces That Offer Savvy Organization (2026)

📅 2021-08-24  |  Read Source →

A desk in a small apartment rarely gets a room of its own. More often it shares a wall with the bed, borrows a corner of the living room, or has to hide somewhere when guests come over. This means the best desks for small spaces really earn their footprint. We’ve rounded up nine that do just that, including a wall desk that puts vertical space to work, a folding desk that collapses to under three inches, and several other petite options that hold their own as furniture, once the laptop is stowed.

Each pick is sized for a compact home office, a bedroom work setup, or an unclaimed nook, and we’ve noted dimensions, materials, and assembly for all of them. If you have a little more room to work with, many have bigger siblings in the same collection.

Branch’s small-space-friendly Daily Desk was designed using what the brand describes as a “nothing unnecessary” approach. Accordingly, what may first appear to be a bare-bones minimalist frame is just the base of a modular system that allows users to opt in to details and accessories that work for them—and say no to the ones that don’t. Among the desk accessories you can add are a felt-lined desk drawer, caster legs, privacy panels, and a headphone stand. Choose from three sizes and six head-turning finish combinations.

If you want a standing desk that doesn’t look like office equipment, turn to Article’s Torme. It adjusts its height with a manual lift mechanism hidden in the top, so you can switch between sitting and standing without a motor or a control panel. And while there are no drawers or additional shelves, a few divided compartments are within the desk’s body to keep your daily essentials close by.

For those interested in a work surface that can double as a vanity or living room sideboard, the Sloane Desk from Castlery is up to the task. Elevated by fluted panels and a duo of discreet storage drawers, its look is polished enough to nimbly complement non-office settings. At just over 47 inches wide, it also has room to work as a small computer desk with a monitor, and the drawers keep chargers and paperwork out of sight when it’s off duty.

This Scandi-style desk is all about taking up vertical space—one of our favorite space-saving hacks. Blending metal and wood in one streamlined design, it provides a top shelf for vertical storage and plenty of wall space to add artwork and decor. Choose between a black or white metal frame to match your home’s mood.

A treat for the eyes—and a space-saving solution for any small home—this compact wood desk pairs its clean, design-forward aesthetic with discreet, functional details: a drawer for extra storage, a hidden cubby for cable management, and a fully finished back that allows it to be displayed anywhere in the room. With solid wood legs and slats, it’s a sturdy home office addition that’s both handsome and heirloom-worthy. At $2,495, it’s the priciest pick here. But it’s also the most likely to outlast the apartment it was bought for.

This electric standing desk changes desk height at the touch of a button. At 34 inches wide, it’s the narrowest standing desk on this list. Available in three sizes and color combinations, its ice-blue finish provides a welcome break from ho-hum neutrals—and, if you’d like, you can order a desk chair in the exact same color for a sleek, monochrome setup.

The ultimate small-space-friendly desk is one that folds away when not in use. The Wohomo folding desk collapses to under three inches, so it can slide behind a door or into a closet between workdays. This Amazon option boasts thousands of positive reviews touting its slim profile, and it’s light enough for one person to move on their own. Available in three sizes (31 inches, 39 inches, and 47 inches), you’re able to choose how much workspace you’ll need. This desk is also equipped with a side hook ideal for keeping your headphones, work bag, or jacket off the ground.

When AD featured Emma Chamberlain’s Los Angeles home in 2022, she was already learning the ropes of decorating with the interiors firm Proem. She has since followed that project with a collection for West Elm. The house taught her to weigh function as heavily as looks. “If something looks great but feels bad, it doesn’t make the cut,” she previously told us. The 36-inch mini desk, shown here in dark walnut, is narrow enough for a bedroom corner or the end of a living room, and at $499 it lands in the middle of this list price-wise. If you have the wall for it, the collection also includes a 48-inch storage desk at $899.

What Are the Best Types of Desks for Small Spaces?

For most small spaces, the best options are wall-mounted desks and floating desks, which keep the floor clear and put vertical space to use. Folding desks are another great option since they’re easy to stow away when you’re done for the day. If you have an empty corner space, a corner desk or a small L-shaped desk can claim square footage that otherwise goes unused. A secretary desk, which closes over your work, makes sense if your home office is also your living room.

How Do I Choose the Right Small Computer Desk for My Needs?

The right computer desk for you will depend on your priorities. If you have multiple monitors, choose a desk with a longer shape and a cord organizer to ensure you have enough surface area. If you work on just a laptop, consider a portable C-shaped desk that can double as a side table when not in use. If your desk is too shallow for a keyboard and a screen, a keyboard tray mounted underneath buys you room without a bigger footprint.

While most sitting desks are between 27 and 30 inches tall, your height will play a factor in ergonomics and how comfortable you’ll feel sitting for long periods of time. For example, if you’re taller, you may find yourself slouching to type. For those who are shorter, your neck could be placed at an odd angle for viewing your laptop or monitor screen. If you’d rather keep the desk you already have, a standing desk converter sits on top of it and gives you an adjustable height without adding another piece of furniture.

Branch, a desk and office-chair company that is focused on ergonomics, recommends that the best desk height maintains a 90-degree angle at your elbows when your arms rest on the desk (a.k.a. while you’re typing). Your desk chair will also affect your comfort and ergonomic posture. You can shop our tested and researched picks in our office chairs guide.

What Are the Most Space-Efficient Desks for Small Apartments?

Vertical wall-mounted desks, foldable desks (basically the Murphy bed of workspaces), and C-shaped portable desks are going to be the most space-efficient options for small apartments. Foldable desks can easily be tucked away when not in use, while C-shaped tables can also function as a drinks table after working hours. If you need a space-saving desk with storage, look for vertical storage above the work surface rather than drawers below it, which keeps the desk’s footprint small.

Where Should I Put a Desk in a Small Apartment?

Start with the power outlets. A desk within reach of a wall plug saves you from running extension cords across the room, and it gives you an obvious place for a phone and laptop charging station. After that, look for natural light and a wall or corner that isn’t already doing another job.

The Best Men’s Rain Boots Ranked From Drizzle to Downpour

📅 2021-08-24  |  Read Source →

The best rain boots for men aren’t a one-size-fits-all shoe. The chance of precipitation may be 100%, but you could be stepping out to a gentle mist, wading through cataclysmic flooding, or taking on sporadic showers—so your choice of shoes and outerwear should adapt to the weather. Popping up out for a quick coffee could require a pair of simple gardening shoes or tall wellingtons depending on whether you’re walking a few blocks or taking your car to a drive-thru.

There are many factors to consider but the most important is that your feet and socks remain dry, no matter the shoe or how heavy the rain is. So take into consideration how tall or short your rain boot ought to be, whether you need grippy soles to get you through some rough terrain or you might have to contend with slush and snow, which inevitably means you’ll need some boots with insulation. Your rain boots and shoes have to contend with a litany of different factors so we rounded up the best options for every kind of wet-weather situation from mild mists to ankle-deep downpours.

For the kind of mediocre rain that has you second-guessing whether you should open your umbrella or just stick it out, you’ll likely get by just fine with a pair of shoes built with a water-resistant or waterproof membrane. Think a slight spray or an annoying mist. Many outdoors-focused shoes and boots come with some type of Gore-tex lining or a coating that helps slough light water. In mild rain situations, your shoes don’t need to be very tall, either. Low-cut gardening shoes or rain-ready sneakers can manage the weather just so long as there’s a low risk of deep puddles along the way.

Norda's trail-ready slip-on runners are an iron-clad pick for any rocky terrain you might encounter, purposely or not. With their lightweight, tear- and water-resistant Dyneema uppers, and crag-grabbing Vibram lugged soles, these bad boys can handle more than a drizzle. Plus, the easy slip-on design is great for when you just need to jam out the door for a quick errand, not to mention for keeping water from sogging your socks.

If the precipitation falls anywhere between a drizzle to moderate showers, I can’t be arsed to bust out my heavier rain boots. I’m unlikely to wear my wellies in showers, too, if I know I won’t be outside for too long. For most situations, a good water-resistant boot will more than suffice. Save your brain cells and just get a pair of Blundstones—they’re tough as nails, reliably dry in the majority of rainy days, and look great with any pant. But if boots on the wellies-chelsea spectrum aren’t your speed, there are plenty of other shoes with rain-prepped details like Gore-tex linings and DWR coatings to keep water at bay.

It's hard to argue against a pair of Blundstones. The quality leather uppers age beautifully, the shape is ruggedly handsome and endlessly styleable, plus the hardy soles can handle most everyday situations regardless of the forecast. No wonder why you see everyone from your local barista to Hollywood's leading men rocking a pair. Their waterproof design is adept at traversing through medium rain, so long as you don't step into any deep puddles, and we like that they don't present as a full-on rubber boot which makes them easier to wear all year long.

What you’re probably imagining when you think of proper rain boots are the kind wrought entirely out of a block of rubber, reaching up to your calf, without any laces. Some people refer to these as Wellingtons—or wellies—whose origins go back to the Duke of Wellington, and though they were originally made of leather, are now mostly thought of as a rain-ready boot. Their seamless, monoblock design in conjunction with impermeable rubber makes them the right choice for heavy rain or if you plan on being outside for several hours in average rainfall conditions. Of course, they’re well-equipped for lighter precipitation as well.

Hunter's flagship boots are a textbook Wellington rain boot. Made entirely of waterproof (not water-resistant) rubber from sole to shaft, no amount of rain, sludge, or slush penetrates these boots. The carved rubber soles keep their grip amidst slippery sidewalks and slick metal grates, while the height is tall enough to stand nice and dry through calf-deep water, but isn't so tall to be a physical or stylistic burden. And at just under $200, they're a reasonable price for the quality.

Style is subjective, we know—that’s the fun of it. But we’re serious about helping our audience get dressed. Whether it’s the best white sneakers, the flyest affordable suits, or the need-to-know menswear drops of the week, GQ Recommends’ perspective is built on years of hands-on experience, an insider awareness of what’s in and what’s next, and a mission to find the best version of everything out there, at every price point.

Our staffers aren’t able to try on every single piece of clothing you read about on GQ.com (fashion moves fast these days), but we have an intimate knowledge of each brand’s strengths and know the hallmarks of quality clothing—from materials and sourcing, to craftsmanship, to sustainability efforts that aren’t just greenwashing. GQ Recommends heavily emphasizes our own editorial experience with those brands, how they make their clothes, and how those clothes have been reviewed by customers. Bottom line: GQ wouldn’t tell you to wear it if we wouldn’t.

We make every effort to cast as wide of a net as possible, with an eye on identifying the best options across three key categories: quality, fit, and price.

To kick off the process, we enlist the GQ Recommends braintrust to vote on our contenders. Some of the folks involved have worked in retail, slinging clothes to the masses; others have toiled for small-batch menswear labels; all spend way too much time thinking about what hangs in their closets.

We lean on that collective experience to guide our search, culling a mix of household names, indie favorites, and the artisanal imprints on the bleeding-edge of the genre. Then we narrow down the assortment to the picks that scored the highest across quality, fit, and price.

Across the majority of our buying guides, our team boasts firsthand experience with the bulk of our selects, but a handful are totally new to us. So after several months of intense debate, we tally the votes, collate the anecdotal evidence, and emerge with a list of what we believe to be the absolute best of the category right now, from the tried-and-true stalwarts to the modern disruptors, the affordable beaters to the wildly expensive (but wildly worth-it) designer riffs.

Whatever your preferences, whatever your style, there's bound to be a superlative version on this list for you. (Read more about GQ's testing process here.)

Pineapple Chaat

📅 2021-04-28  |  Read Source →

“Chaats are a mighty snack that tell India’s culinary story more adeptly than any other food,” writes Nashville-based Indian chef Maneet Chauhan in her cookbook Chaat. She reminisces about a fruity street snack sold by a chaat vendor near the Hyderabadi train station. Sweet pineapple, tangy lime juice, sour-salty chaat masala, chile powder, cilantro, and pomegranate seeds created what she describes as “a decadent mosaic of colors and plucky flavors.” Chauhan suggests swapping in ripe mango or watermelon when in season, or drizzling the finished dish with lightly salted yogurt for added richness and depth. Serve as a snack, or as a side dish for barbecued meats or seafood.

Adapted from Chaat: Recipes from the Kitchens, Markets, and Railways of India by Maneet Chauhan and Jody Eddy. Copyright © 2020. Available from Clarkson Potter.

Featured in “Our Favorite Fruit Snack” by Stephanie Burt.

Chef Maneet Chauhan takes her cues from the street vendors near the Hyderabadi train station for this sweet-and-salty fruit snack.

The 8 Best Refrigerators to Keep Your Food Fresh and Organized

📅 2021-03-25  |  Read Source →

We gathered the latest picks from popular brands like Samsung, Maytag, and more.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

A refrigerator is intended to do only three things: preserve food, store it properly, and supply enough lighting to see all contents. We also want our fridge to be reliable and last a long time so we don’t have to go through this exhaustive shopping exercise again until it’s absolutely necessary.

When shopping for a refrigerator, we're not just looking for something that keeps our food cold. We require aesthetic appeal from our primary kitchen appliance. We also crave convenience in the form of spacious and adjustable shelves and bins, temperature or humidity-controlled spaces, an ice maker so we can ditch the refillable trays, and whatever other clever additions fridge makers come up with to lure us into spending more cash.

If you haven’t shopped for a refrigerator in a while, we’ve got some good news and some bad news. The bad news is that refrigerators may be more expensive due to ongoing tariffs on both foreign steel and aluminum used to manufacture models in the U.S. and on wholly imported fridges. The good news, though, is that there is a wider variety of designs and sizes to match your family’s specific needs, plus features like exterior or interior water and ice dispensing options, separate food-specific cooling spaces, and an abundance of smart tech.

To help you find the perfect fridge for your kitchen, we researched a variety of options based on specific criteria and found the best of the best. We also made sure our picks all varied in terms of style, so below, you'll find our top recommendations for French door refrigerators, side-by-side fridges, and a number of other types, as well.

As with any other appliance, our consideration for best models was primarily based on value—capacity and features for the money. Value also incorporates the reputation of the manufacturer and opinions from both retailers and consumers (from online reviews). We also compared capacities and feature sets of various models and took into account our own hands-on experiences at appliance stores and trade shows over the years.

When faced with seemingly equal choices, especially on price, we always leaned toward higher-capacity models since we know there’s no such thing as too much refrigerator space. However, we also recognize that higher-capacity refrigerators are usually full or standard depth models that may be too big for many kitchens, so we’ve tried to add less pricey, smaller alternatives where we found them.

Stewart Wolpin wrote the initial version of this article—he's covered consumer technology, including home entertainment and appliances, since the early 1980s. Amber Joglar, a technology and home appliance editor with PM, handled the latest update to this roundup.

While there is a plethora of premium refrigerators priced $5,000 and up—and some priced $15,000 and more—we decided to keep our “Best” choices under $5,000.

If ensuring your food stays cool is at the top of your list when shopping for a refrigerator, then your best bet is investing in this four-door Samsung model. It's equipped with AI hybrid cooling, which essentially means using just electricity, sans refrigerant, via a semiconductor-based component.

This fridge automatically cools down to compensate for when you leave the door open too long. As a result, Samsung claims that meat and seafood are preserved a smidge longer, and your fridge’s overall power usage can be lowered by up to 10 percent.

Beyond this cooling technology, the Samsung offers plenty of convenient features. The beverage center inside the left top door provides a tall water dispenser, a 48-ounce autofill water pitcher with infuser, and a removable nozzle for cleaning. The dual ice maker behind the bottom left door makes both ice cubes and ice spheres.

The fridge’s doors open via gentle touch rather than forcing you to tug on handles, which is useful for when your arms are filled with groceries. You can also set the multi-pantry drawer for the correct cooling conditions to properly preserve fruit and veggies, beverages, or meat and fish.

This Samsung counter-depth four-door has a generous 22.5 cubic feet of capacity and 13.8 percent more cooling capability than previous comparable Samsung models.

This GE fridge is about as basic and uncomplicated as it gets, which explains why it only costs around $800 after discount. What makes this fridge a bargain is its substantial 21.9 cubic feet of storage space, which is more than a majority of other entry-level top-freezer fridges we considered.

One of its door shelves accommodates gallon jugs, and the included bins are modular to adjust to different jar, bottle, and container heights. Temperature controls are straightforward and easy to access on the roof of the fridge. It also includes a Sabbath mode to eliminate all the fridge’s electrical responses when the door is opened. Plus, it’s garage-ready.

This model is available in five finishes, but any colors other than white add to the overall cost. It also lacks both a freezer light and an ice maker, which means you’re going to have to fill and empty trays somewhat regularly.

But if you want to avoid an ice-making mess, you can buy an optional ice maker designed for this model, although it will occupy more than a third of your freezer space. (Or you could buy one of our favorite countertop nugget ice makers instead.)

This is one robust fridge for just $1,200. You get an impressive 27.4 cubic feet of total storage space in this full-depth Samsung stainless steel side-by-side, plus the interior ice maker is located in the door to create even more freezer shelf space.

Aside from being roomy, this fridge includes a raft of both cooling and convenience features normally found on more expensive models. For instance, it instantly lowers the temperature of your fridge and freezer section to quickly bring recently purchased goods to their appropriate storage levels.

The in-door water dispenser also dispenses both cubed and crushed ice, but it lacks a light, which makes it difficult to accurately fill a glass in a dark kitchen. There's an LCD panel on the inside of the fridge for monitoring refrigerator and freezer temperatures, ice maker filter status, and managing lock controls.

Multi-vent cooling lowers the temp in every corner of the fridge, which prevents ice-infused liquids. While it lacks some of the fancier frills of some of our other, more expensive choices, this Samsung provides a lot of storage space, especially in its freezer, for a relatively low price tag.

If it's even possible for a refrigerator to be entertaining, this French door LG does it and thankfully it doesn't skimp on functionality in the process. Right off the bat, you’ll notice a shaded window on the right door—just gently knock twice and it turns clear so you can take a peek inside. Then, grab the door just right, and a door within the main door opens with easy, quick access to snacks and drinks without letting all the cool air out from the main fridge compartment.

If you’re tired of old-fashioned ice cubes, freshen drinks with Craft Ice, an interior ice maker that fashions slow-melting spheric ice modules. The exterior water dispenser also makes both crushed and cubed ice, and the dispenser/ice maker mechanism inside the left door is thin enough to accommodate three door bins.

Unfortunately, the freezer is organized with deep, basket-style compartments, which makes it difficult to quickly find what you need in a pinch. But if you’re a frequent party host, the freezer really steps up the ice production, so you’re less likely to ever run out, even during a lively party.

There are side-by-side fridges with fancier features, but you'd be hard-pressed to find a better deal than this Whirlpool model, considering its combination of capacity, extras, aesthetic appeal, and overall cooling excellence.

This fridge has a spacious 28.7-cubic-foot capacity and is equipped with both an extra tall exterior water and ice dispenser to accommodate all sizes of glasses, bottles, and pitchers. The interior ice bin is installed on the door to free up freezer storage space. There's a good variety of glass shelves inside, and they extend wall-to-wall to truly maximize all fridge and freezer space available.

There is also a temperature management system that senses and adapts to keep food fresh. Special pantry and deli drawers maintain organization, too. If any foods have defrosted in between the grocery store and home, the freezer has a Fast Freeze option that quickly drops the temperature 10 degrees lower than the normal setting, so your foods can get back to fully frozen in a jiffy.

Unfortunately, if you’re looking to match this fridge with your kitchen decor, it's available only in a “stainless” finish.

This full-depth GE bottom freezer, which has been available since 2022, is still well-respected in its category, and it costs around $1,000 less than it was at first launch. Its enduring popularity can likely be attributed to its 24.9-cubic-foot capacity that’s generous for this class of fridge, although the water filter is unusually bulky and takes up a little more cooling space than we would have liked.

Fortunately, it includes an ice maker along with a larger-than-average storage bin—perfect for party preparation. A turbo cool setting automatically drops the internal temperature if the door’s been open too long. We also love the unique, horizontally-sliding snack drawer that easily moves across the fridge to make room for taller items below.

Other nice features include touch temperature controls, an open door alarm, and a fingerprint-resistant stainless finish. And, as found on most top- or bottom-freezer models, the top door is reversible, so you can install it to swing open left or right.

This model is also Energy Star-certified, meaning it meets strict standards set by the EPA and uses between 9 and 15 percent less energy than models that have not been rated.

Samsung makes arguably the smartest fridges around, particularly with its Family Hub models that integrate seamlessly into smart home ecosystems. The right door of this appliance boasts a 32-inch vertical touch screen that provides access to recipes, shopping lists, ordering, and other food-related tasks. In addition to these functions, it acts a streaming interface and display for your kids’ school drawings or home videos.

Inside, this fridge has a camera that recognizes and automatically labels 37 unobscured fresh food items such as fruits and vegetables, or you can do this manually. On the left side, there’s a double quick-access door. Behind that lives a beverage center designed with both a regular water dispenser and an auto-refill water pitcher with an infuser.

Open its doors either manually or automatically by touching a sensor. To better preserve different types of food, one of the freezer drawers lets you set one of five different temperatures depending on what you’re storing. A dual ice maker makes both standard cubes and smaller “ice bits,” but not crushed ice. An internal camera lets you see what’s inside your fridge—or not—when shopping via the smartphone app, which also lets you monitor the fridge’s power usage.

GE Profile appliances are considered high-end within the GE family, sitting above standard GE appliances but below its relatively expensive Monogram line. To put it simply, you get luxury features without a large price tag by investing in this model.

Inside the left of the four-door refrigerator is an auto-refillable water pitcher that doubles as a dispenser, and the bottom left freezer compartment contains roomy storage that holds a whopping 12.5 pounds of filtered ice. The bottom right can also function as a freezer, chiller, or fridge if you run out of space up top.

This fridge doesn't have an exterior water dispenser, but it does have a built-in pitcher with advanced filtration inside the left door. The door-in-door with rotating bin is a standout feature for us, too.

From a design POV, you get a smooth stainless steel exterior door finish with sleek handles, and the interior is highlighted by silver shelf and drawer trim handles, and an LED light wall. If you want to take advantage of smart features, the fridge has built-in WiFi and compatibility with the SmartHQ app for a fully customizable experience.

Each fridge form offers varying cubic-foot capacity, usually expressed as total space. Until recently, inside capacity was somewhat restricted by the need for refrigerant circulation and insulation inside a fridge’s thick walls. But many newer models with updated cooling and insulation technologies have slimmed these inside walls to create so-called “max,” “mega,” or “XL” capacity models, creating more inside capacity without increasing exterior dimensions.

Bear in mind, some vendors fudge on capacity, often rounding up to the nearest whole number in the name of the fridge. For instance, a “23” cubic foot model may actually only contain 22.5 cubic feet of storage.

So how do you decide how big your new fridge needs to be? Obviously, how much capacity you can buy depends on how much space you have in your kitchen for a refrigerator.

Figure 4- to 6 cubic feet per family member, leaning toward the higher end for a lot of shared items. Alternatively, you can start with ten cubic feet for the household, then add 1.5 cubic feet per household member to get a minimum capacity.

In terms of fridge-freezer split, most models assign 65 to 70 percent of space to cooling and the remaining 30 to 35 percent to freezing. If you’re a bulk buyer or frequent meal preparer, we recommend you find a model with more freezer space.

You want shelves and inside door bins to offer varying height adjustments to accommodate the tallest types of containers such as opened wine bottles. Some fridges offer shelves that slide out and door bins that can accommodate bulky gallon jugs or tilt out for easier access.

All refrigerators include storage drawers, but the best are those models that offer drawers with some sort of temperature and/or humidity control. When shopping, check the drawers to make sure they slide in and out smoothly, come out easily for cleaning, and don’t feel cheap.

Refrigerators with twin doors—side-by-side and French—are the only models in which you’ll find exterior in-door water and ice dispensers. Most in-door water dispensers also dispense ice—often both cubed and crushed—but some don’t offer ice, so double-check this before you buy. If a twin-door model doesn’t include an exterior water dispenser, there’s likely one inside the fridge.

If you’re replacing a fridge without an in-door water dispensing with one that does, you (or, more likely, a plumber) will likely first have to install a separate water line that connects from your under-sink pipes to your new refrigerator. You’ll also occasionally have to change the refrigerator’s water filter—usually around twice a year—but ask first to make sure of the timing. Our advice: order in a supply of filters so you’re sure you’re always dispensing the freshest filtered water.

A growing number of “smart” refrigerators include interior cameras to let you view your fridge’s contents to confirm if you need something while grocery shopping. A camera-equipped smart model with a smart screen on the door can also scan your remaining ingredients and create an edible recipe, assemble a shopping list and create an order from a grocery delivery service, and even identify what food items have reached their expiration date.

Yes, refrigerators still come in basic white, but you also can choose from a growing variety of subtleties such as gray and black tones in shiny or matte finishes (with smudge- or finger-resistant coatings). But a few fridge makers also offer custom-made colors, including finishes to match your kitchen cabinets or decor.

Do note that a model listed as having a “stainless” finish does not necessarily mean you’re getting an actual stainless steel door. In most instances, just the word “stainless” likely means the door is some other material, not stainless steel. To make sure you’re getting a true stainless steel door, first feel it, as stainless steel is cooler to the touch than other materials. Also, take along a refrigerator magnet—most stainless steel doors are not magnetic.

There are, essentially, only five forms of fridges, all defined by how the doors and drawers are arranged. In order of price, features available, and capacity (low to high), the five fridge forms are:

This is the most familiar no-frills fridge format with two doors: a top freezer compartment and a larger fresh food compartment below. Top freezer fridges are usually the least expensive fridge option, landing somewhere between $500-$900. They also range between 10 to 24 cubic feet of space, and are best suited for single folks or couples on a budget, or as a second fridge for a recreational basement or garage.

No frills means you’re unlikely to find any top freezer models with in-door water dispensers or an ice maker. Most models come with reversible doors that open left-to-right or right-to-left, however.

A.k.a. “bottom freezer” models, these refrigerators are equipped with a single door up top and a freezer drawer usually encompassing two shelving tiers below. Bottom freezer models are presumably more attractive to those with fewer freezer needs since you have to kneel or bend over to rummage through it.

Bottom freezer models generally provide a bit more freezer space than a top freezer— around 18 to 22 cubic feet of total capacity—and typically cost more than a top freezer model. Like a top freezer model, you’re unlikely to find bottom freezer models with in-door water dispensers, but some pricier versions do include ice makers.

Rather than a horizontal freezer, side-by-side models offer vertical freezing and cooling compartments. Side-by-side interior horizontal space, however, is narrower than a top- or bottom-freezer model, so wide platter trays or frozen pizzas may not fit. Side-by-sides are more likely to offer in-door water or ice dispensing, which can further reduce freezer space.

Essentially a side-by-side with a bottom freezer, except the two top French doors open out to reveal a single large and wide cooling compartment. Since there’s no wall dividing the top section, French door models offer the highest capacity—up to around 30 cubic feet worth of fresh food room—which makes these the best option for a large and active family.

French door models usually include either in-door or interior water and ice dispensers. Higher capacity, of course, means higher prices—French door models start at around $1,400 depending on interior space and features, such as more convenient separate dual freezer drawers.

Four-door French-door fridges replace the bottom freezer drawer(s) with freezer doors that open into separate compartments. However, you’ll see models with French doors with dual drawer freezers referred to as “four-door,” which they are not since a drawer is decidedly not a door. More expensive four-door models allow you to convert one of the freezer sections into an extra refrigerator compartment if you need extra cooling space.

Most refrigerators last around seven years. Spending a bit more now on a better model means you can go a lot longer without having to buy a replacement. Models with long warranties usually signal a longer expected lifespan.

Our general refrigerator shopping advice: you already know your current refrigerator’s shortcomings (that’s presumably why you’re looking to buy a new one). Match your known needs with the features outlined above that you find attractive before you go to a store and are confronted by a pushy salesperson.

It's not recommended to turn your fridge on and off for short periods of time because it can actually increase wear on the compressor, but powering down during longer absences helps save energy and prevents issues like mold.

Before heading out for vacation or an extended trip, you should remove all food, clean and dry the interior, unplug the appliance, and keep the doors propped open for air circulation.
