These 10 Brooks Running Shoes Will Help You Run Your Best Miles Yet

📅 2018-05-21  |  Read Source →

From the Ghost to the Glycerin, Brooks is our go-to brand for comfiest trainers.

We earn a commission for products purchased through some links in this article. Why Trust Us?

Editor’s Note, November 4, 2026: Brooks has a mega sale going on right now. The Glycerin Flex, Adrenaline GTS 25, and Glycerin Max 2 are on discounts as high as 25% off. The Adrenaline GTS 26 is out now—we’ll be getting in feedback soon. All this is to say, better stock up on the 25 before it’s gone.

In 1977, the Vantage was the first Brooks shoe to top our charts. The running renaissance began several years prior, when Frank Shorter won the 1972 Olympic marathon in Munich. Two years later, the shoe industry would be changed forever with the introduction of ethylene vinyl acetate (EVA) foam, which was lighter, softer, and had more rebound than the rubbers used in midsoles around that time. Our editors knew Brooks had a winner when we tested the Vantage.

Almost half a century later, the brand is still dazzling us with its innovations and earning prizes in the 2026 Runner’s World Shoe Awards. And to think the company started out more than a hundred years ago manufacturing ballet slippers and bath shoes.

Since the 1970s, Brooks has solely focused on upping the ante in shoe tech. The Vantage also introduced the varus wedge, which was basically a medial post used to help with instability caused by overpronation. Now the varus wedge is dated and has been replaced with Brooks’s “GuideRails” system. EVA is also being phased out with improved foams that are bouncier and even softer.

Gear Up for Training Season: Best Running Shorts ● Best Socks for Runners ● Best Running Watches

Our team vets hundreds of shoes a year and distributes models to more than 250 local testers for evaluation. Shoe testers of different levels, ages, arch heights, and gait patterns log at least a hundred miles before submitting feedback on a shoe’s fit, feel, and ride. Keep returning for more updates as we make our way through 2026’s hottest Brooks releases.

All weights are based on men’s 9 and women’s 7 unless otherwise noted.

The Glycerin 23 knocked the Ghost 18 out of Best Overall this round. While the Ghost 18 is still on this list as Best Daily Trainer, we found during testing that the Glycerin 23 is more foot shape-friendly. Testers liked the broader platform and toe room, as well as the bouncy and supportive cushioning. The shoe is my go-to for daily training—but the long run is where it shines.

I usually grab a carbon-plate shoe for my long runs, especially when I’m less confident about being out on the road for two hours at the beginning of marathon training. But the Glycerin 23’s midsole upgrade convinced me to save my carbon shoes for race day. DNA Tuned is lighter than DNA Loft, the Glycerin’s previous foam, and delivers a springier ride. My legs feel fresh when I power down hills in the shoes. The energy return doesn’t make my double-digit runs feel like a crawl.

The shoe is also available as the Glycerin GTS 23, the stability counterpart. I find the Glycerin GTS 23 slightly stiffer and firmer than the neutral model, but it’s a good choice for runners who need a little more support on their long runs if they’re prone to late-onset overpronation, or if they prefer denser cushioning underfoot.

Shop Men’s at brooksrunning.com | Shop Women’s at brooksrunning.com

The Launch is a solid speedwork shoe for $120. It has the Hyperion Elite’s original DNA Flash v2 in the forefoot. The nitrogen-infused foam was what Brooks elite athletes relied on before the brand switched to DNA Gold PEBA.

The Launch 12 is lightweight and narrow. Wide-foot runners will want to opt for a more accommodating speed shoe, like the Hyperion 3 on this list. All other runners who have a standard to narrow foot shape will appreciate this bargain shoe’s swiftness on the track and road.

“They felt light enough to rip off some faster-paced fartlek segments during the middle of a regular run, but still supportive enough to make it through a longer run,” said tester and Hyper Editor Ashley Tysiac.

Shop Men’s at brooksrunning.com | Shop Women’s at brooksrunning.com

For the first time, a Ghost’s midsole has PEBA—the soft and bouncy foam that once was reserved for marathon racing shoes is making its way into trainers. The new DNA Hype foam is a combination of PEBA and EVA, and the difference in ride is noticeable comparing it to the Ghost 18 (below).

Our testers liked the high energy return on their runs, causing them to favor the shoe for hard tempos and track workouts. I took the Ghost Amp out on a few 400-meter intervals on the track and appreciated its generous cushioning and high rebound ride as I pounded each lap.

“The midsole hits a sweet spot that’s neither too cushioned nor too firm,” said Senior Editor Cat Bowen. “It has decent rebound and springiness, and it powers through miles whether you’re just hitting distance or working in tempo runs or other speedwork.”

Shop Men’s at brooksrunning.com | Shop Women’s at brooksrunning.com

The Ghost always hits the right marks for every kind of runner at every level—soft, supportive cushioning; all-encompassing comfort; not too light, not too heavy. The Brooks team kept this winning formula. Just like the Ghost 17, the newest iteration has DNA Loft v3 foam, which was used in the Glycerin 21 and Glycerin GTS 21.

However, testers were divisive with the Ghost 18’s key update. The new jacquard engineered air mesh upper gives the Ghost a sporty meets street casual look. A flat-knit tongue is padded for comfort and provides a secure fit. Our women testers experienced hot spots, especially below their big toe in the bunion-prone area of their foot.

“The length was great; the width not so much,” said Deputy Editor Mallory Creveling. “But the upper material was also great; loved the stretch. And the cushioning underfoot also felt good and smooth. But that forefoot area did feel crammed the more I ran.”

Runner-in-Chief Jeff Dengate had a different experience: “Brooks continues to nail the fit. It’s one of the reasons I love their shoes—no matter how a model evolves, I know I’m going to get a good wrap. Even in the Ghost, which is a little more spartan for padding and luxury, it still wraps the foot well and feels good no matter how far I run.”

The Ghost 18 is a great trainer for new runners and those looking for a trusty workhorse to log daily mileage. However, in lieu of the Ghost 18, women and wide-foot runners may want to opt for our Best Overall, the Glycerin 23, for their daily training, instead.

Shop Men’s at brooksrunning.com | Shop Women’s at brooksrunning.com

Before you come at recommending an “illegal shoe” that goes well above World Athletics below-40mm guidelines, know this: marathons are hard, and the majority of athletes running them aren’t 2:30-elites—they’re runners like me and you.

A shoe like the Hyperion Max 4 has everything you need to make the marathon less of an hours-long suffer-fest. A nylon plate helps with snappy turnover and feels more forgiving for runners who may not have enough power running on rigid carbon. Thick slabs of PEBA DNA Gold foam and DNA Flash v2 cushion your ride, helping you endure miles of pavement pounding.

Two major draws of the Hyperion Max 4: the rubber coverage on the outsole and the broader platform. Super shoes tend to skimp on rubber to save weight. Though the Hyperion Max 4 weighs more than your average racing shoe, the bouncy super foams make your runs feel effortless. And the rubber outsole provides something underrated: confidence. Running in the Hyperion Max 4, you won’t fear for your life turning sharp corners or slipping over yellow strips. The wide midfoot area provides even more stability and also makes for a more accommodating fit if you have wide feet.

Shop Men’s at brooksrunning.com | Shop Women’s at brooksrunning.com

After adding a full PEBA midsole to the Hyperion Elite 5, Brooks zeroed in on everything else in the next iteration of the shoe. The Hyperion Elite 6 rides more aggressively thanks to its new geometry. The SpeedVault+ plate and RapidRoll rocker are adjusted to promote faster turnover, making those mid-race surges smooth—especially when you’re low on gas.

Our testers called out its accommodating fit in the midfoot area, saying it isn’t as narrow as other racing shoes they’ve tested. They appreciated the softness and comfort of the midsole as well as its propulsive bounce at marathon pace.

“This shoe is the most comfortable race day super shoe for maximizing performance in workouts and race scenarios in the half marathon distance or greater,” said tester Sean Phillips. “I found this shoe to be lighter, with slightly better grip on the rubber outsole.”

The Glycerin Flex changes the paradigm of cushioning as we know it. For one thing, its stack height is comparably low at 36mm for a cushioned trainer in a sea of high-stacked super trainers reaching beyond 40mm. The tooling on the midsole is also unlike anything we’ve seen. A diagonal midfoot Flex Zone allows you to bend the shoe in half in your hands. The pliability is meant to mimic how your foot is built, allowing your feet to flex instead of being forced to comply with stiff shoe construction.

The DNA Tuned cushioning is the same in the Glycerin Max and Glycerin 23. Small cells in the forefoot and large ones in the heel create a luxurious ride that absorbs shock without the foamy bulk. Lightweight and malleable, it challenges what we think when we hear cushioned trainer.

“The Brooks Glycerin Flex strikes an effective balance between lightweight construction, plush cushioning, flexible midsole, comfortable fit, and outsole durability, making it well suited as an everyday trainer,” said tester Lindsay Vuong. “The shoe transitions easily from running to gym sessions and daily wear. I especially appreciate the padded heel, which surrounds the back of my foot like a pillow while still holding it securely in place.”

Shop Men’s at brooksrunning.com | Shop Women’s at brooksrunning.com

The Adrenaline GTS turns 25, and it’s plusher and more responsive than its forebears. Just like the Ghost 17, Brooks added 1mm of cushioning to the heel and a whopping 3mm to the forefoot, making the shoe have a 10mm drop. This update packs it with more nitrogen-infused DNA Loft v3 cushioning and provides a more balanced platform.

Brooks’s guide rails on the lateral and medial sides of the shoe act as bumpers to align ankle and knee movement. This holistic approach to stability makes the shoe a great pick for overpronators and for neutral runners who need a little support later in their runs when their form gets sloppy This is why the Adrenaline is a long run favorite.

“I like that it felt like a normal trainer,” said Associate Service Editor Matt Rudisill, who runs with a neutral gait. “I thought these were going to be clunky and solid. I was pleasantly surprised that they were so comfortable, light, and still had that stable base that you want in a supportive shoe.”

Tester and overpronator Chris Garges also had praise for the Adrenaline GTS 25.

“I’ve run in just about every model of this shoe, many of them predating the guide rails system,” he said. “This shoe provides the stability that I need as a heel striking over pronator. I had no issues with the stability even later into a 14-mile training run.”

The Glycerin Max 2 provides a luxurious, max-cushioned ride. Testers who previously ran in the original Glycerin Max gave mixed feedback on the second model. They expected a squishy soft, relatively flexible high-stacked shoe. This version is stiffer; the shoe is meant for easy days and recovery miles.

“The cushioning, which is overkill 95 percent of the time, was quite pleasant on the slowest of slow days,” said tester Craig Wallace. “When my legs were tired and ready for an easy run, these shoes are a go-to.”

Matthew Fronheiser, a heel-striker, praised the Glycerin Max 2’s overall comfort, saying the upper “seriously rocks.” He liked its padded fit and breathability. He also appreciated the cushioned ride. The Glycerin Max 2 has Brooks’s DNA Tuned midsole foam and a GlideRoll profile, which promotes smooth turnover.

“Would I grab this shoe for a relaxed 8- to 10-mile run?” said Fronheiser. “One hundred times yes!”

Brooks added 4mm of nitrogen-infused DNA Loft v2 cushioning to the Cascadia 20 to provide even more protection and comfort running on the trail. Testers said the Vibram Megagrip outsole was a favorite feature, its 4mm lugs effectively bit into loose surfaces, handling mud and gravel well.

The Trail Adapt system—which involves a wide base, flexibility in key areas, and adaptable lugs—makes the Cascadia 20 a favorite shoe pick for runners who need more stability off-road.

A minor drawback: the lack of airflow. Testers found the shoe’s upper comfy but said the it ran hot. Despite the need for more ventilation, they described the Cascadia 20 as a solid trail shoe for most surfaces.

Brooks has categorized its shoes to help runners choose the best pair that fits their running style and preferred experience. I’ve been a Brooks die-hard since my high school days. For the past 20 years, I’ve been keeping tabs on product updates and saying goodbye to discontinued lineups (taps for the PureProject minimalist list). Here’s a breakdown of everything you need to know—foams, stability tech—about Brooks’s footwear.

One helpful way to choose Brooks shoes is by knowing Brooks’s foams. The kind of foam determines the type of running experience the shoe is meant for.

Through the decades, Brooks revved up its EVA foam, developing MoGo foam in 2006, and then DNA foam in 2013. Known for its adaptive midsole cushioning, DNA has undergone an evolution of its own, breaking into its own subcategories in types of shoes.

Runners looking for a more cushioned shoe will want to try trainers with DNA Loft midsoles. DNA Loft v3, for example, which is featured in the Ghost 18 and the Adrenaline GTS 25, is nitrogen-infused EVA and rubber. It has large cells of air to pack in cushioning without adding extra weight.

At the beginning of 2025, Brooks released the Glycerin Max which showcased DNA Tuned. In developing DNA Tuned, the brand’s team found they could manipulate the cells creating larger cells in the heel and smaller cells in the forefoot. In an interview with Runner’s World, Brooks Chief Product Officer Carson Caprara explained how this dual-cell cushioning softens landings and becomes a springboard for responsive toe-off.

For runners looking for shoes for speedwork or racing, they should look for shoes with DNA Flash or DNA Gold; the latter is a pure PEBA midsole. DNA Hype is a combination of EVA and PEBA. We found in our testing its good for speedwork.

Before the Adrenaline GTS launched in 1999, Brooks had set out to build off of the success of the Addiction. Runners wanted a more flexible version of the stability shoe, and so, the Adrenaline GTS—Go-to-Shoe—was born.

In 2018, Brooks adapted a more holistic approach with stability, implementing a guide rails system into its shoes. The guide rails system has two dense foam pieces in the midsole, one on the medial side and one on the lateral side. These foam pieces reduce your foot from rolling outward and inward when your form becomes erratic due to overpronation or late onset overpronation.

Now redefined as Go-to-Support, GTS Brooks shoes are the stability counterpart of their neutral models. In this way, you can easily find your favorite Brooks trainer with just a little extra support in the midsole and not much else changed with the shoe.

Guide rails offer unobtrusive support for both overpronators and neutral runners.

Our lab tests show that DNA Loft remains Brooks’s softest midsole material.

Screen-printed mesh provides lightweight structure with more airflow and flexibility.

This sticky outsole compound aids grip and wet-surface traction on trail shoes.

The most responsive expression of Brooks’s DNA foam debuted on the Hyperion Tempo.

What’s the best Brooks shoe for beginning runners?

We always recommend the Ghost because it provides plenty of supportive cushioning without being too bulky. It’s an ideal daily trainer for beginners to start with as an offshoot.

How does the Hyperion Elite 6 compare to other carbon shoes?

According to testers, the Hyperion Elite 6 feels less softer and more accommodating compared to other super shoes they’ve tested. The new DNA Gold midsole adds more edge compared to the Hyperion Elite’s DNA Flash foams, with testers saying the rebound is comparable to the Adidas Adizero Adios Pro 4 or Saucony Endorphin Pro 5.

Does Brooks have bargain-priced shoes and how do they stack up to more expensive models?

The Launch 12, included on this list, has a fit and feel almost on par with more expensive models. It’s not as responsive compared to $140 to $160 trainers due to its EVA cushioning, but it delivers a smooth ride.

6 Ways to Stop Chimney Fires Before They Start

📅 2017-10-11  |  Read Source →

Updated Nov 5, 2026 12:46 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

There are more than 25,000 chimney fires incurring $125 million-plus in property damage every year in the United States. That damage is largely due to flames in the lower chimney migrating upward to crack, warp, melt, or otherwise negatively affect the masonry or metal chimney walls. In the most severe cases, chimney fires can destroy houses and put lives at risk. These tragedies are often preventable: Failure to regularly inspect, repair, and clean a chimney can cause it to malfunction or collect dangerous buildup that puts your family at risk.

If you practice these prevention tips, that hard-working column above your fireplace or wood stove should continue to do its job of ushering smoke and other by-products out of your home, so you and yours can safely cozy up in front of the flames. Ahead, learn what causes chimney fires and steps you can take to prevent them.

The main culprit of chimney fires is creosote. This highly flammable, dark brown substance coats chimney walls when by-products of a fire (smoke, vapor, and unburned wood) condense as they move from the hot fireplace or wood stove into the cooler chimney. If the temperature in the chimney flue (the space inside the chimney) is high enough, and the creosote build-up thick enough, creosote can catch fire—and that fire can spread and move up the flue.

To minimize creosote build-up, only burn seasoned hardwood that has dried for at least six months and contains a moisture content of no more than 20 percent, which you can easily test with a wood moisture meter. And remember to always keep the damper (a metal plate in the flue that regulates the draft) open during a fire to maintain adequate airflow.

And if your chimney’s flue liner (the layer between the flue and chimney walls) is uninsulated, it’s recommended to insulate it by either wrapping a heat-resistant insulation blanket around the liner or pouring an insulation mix like vermiculite into the space between the flue liner and flue. An insulated flue liner will prevent flue temperatures from getting too cool—a condition that could encourage fire by-products to condense and form creosote.

Because many defects that lead to chimney fires, such as a cracked flue liner, aren’t visible to the naked eye, it’s crucial to have a Chimney Safety Institute of America-certified chimney sweep inspect your fireplace or woodstove, chimney, and venting system once a year. This pro will inform you of any damage and, if desired, repair it as well as remove soot, creosote, or obstructions such as bird nests.

There are three levels of chimney inspections:

Consult your chimney sweep company about which inspection level is recommended for your chimney and venting system.

If when you scratch a finger against a chimney wall and uncover one-eighth of an inch of build-up, it’s time for a cleaning. A professional chimney cleaning costs between $100 and $350 and usually includes a sweep of the outside of the chimney along with the firebox, the smoke shelf, the smoke chamber above the firebox, the flue, and the flue liner. However, budget-conscious DIYers willing to get up on a ladder can also clean their chimney without professional help by using a wide chimney brush.

This creosote destroyer coats your chimney liner with protective chemicals while you burn, preventing creosote buildup. Read our full review.

Leaves and animal nests inside a flue can quickly fuel a chimney fire when touched by loose embers from a fire. A chimney cap on the crown around the outside opening of the flue will keep debris and critters out. The cap will also prevent “back puffing,” whereby escaped smoke from a fire re-enters the chimney and then the home. Caps also prevent acidic rainwater from entering and corroding the chimney. Expect to spend anywhere from $50 for galvanized metal caps to $500 or more for decorative models. While some homeowners opt to put in a chimney cap themselves, self-installation may void the warranty—it may be worth it to call a pro.

Always stick to the best fire starters when selecting fuel, kindling, and tinder.

Low-temperature, slow-burning fires, particularly those left to smolder overnight, produce more smoke and leave behind more unburned combustible material. When that hardens into creosote on the chimney walls, there’s an increased risk of chimney fires. Hot, fast-burning fires, on the other hand, leave far less smoke, vapor, and unburned wood behind, so little to no creosote forms.

The best way to burn a clean fire is to use the top-down burn method: Place the large logs vertically at the bottom of the fireplace or wood stove (with the bottoms of the logs facing you), add four to five horizontal layers of kindling, then top with tinder and light.

And, before retiring for the night, always extinguish the fire: Spread out the wood and embers with a fireplace poker, then cover them with the ash lying at the bottom of the fireplace using a fireplace shovel. Then douse the cooled wood and embers with enough baking soda to cover them completely—the sodium bicarbonate will extinguish any remaining embers. After the firebox cools (for a minimum of three hours, but preferably eight), shovel the ashes into a metal container. Fill the metal container with water and store it outside the home away from other flammable materials until you’re ready to discard them.

After months of scouring the market and putting products through their paces, we’ve named the best of the best in new tools. There’s something for everyone, from veteran pros to average Joes.

Bob Vila is America’s Handyman. As a TV host, author, builder and historic preservationist, he has been heavily involved in construction and remodeling for the better part of a half century.

The 7 Best Electric Chainsaws of 2026

📅 2017-10-11  |  Read Source →

Cordless battery-powered chainsaws are quiet, light, and fast—everything you need for a spring refresh.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

If you own a home with trees on your property, an electric chainsaw is a must-have tool in your yard care arsenal. Electric chainsaws have come a long way and, in most cases, closed the gap to their gas-powered cousins—they’re easy to start (just push a button or two in most cases), generate little noise, produce no fumes, and simplify maintenance. We’ve tested electric saws as big as 20 inches and they’re powerful enough to bring down a mature tree.

Saws with 24-inch bars or more are still considered gas engine territory, but that’s overkill for most homeowners. For cleaning up after storms, felling small to medium-size trees, and regular pruning or arboreal maintenance, an electric chainsaw is the ideal choice.

We noted how well each chainsaw endured prolonged use. Making repeated cuts can cause a chainsaw, and its battery, to heat up. If it gets too hot, it will trigger a thermal limit and the saw’s overload protection will shut it down. We also considered comfort, vibration, saw balance, and ease of use to make our final recommendations.

Stihl’s new battery-powered MSA 260 C-O tops our list of chainsaws for two main reasons: run-time and constant, consistent power. These are the two most common concerns or complaints we hear from people when it comes to electric saws. The MSA 260 C-O made significantly more cuts on one battery charge than any other saw tested. But that’s only partly attributable to the saw—it’s also due to Stihl’s new AllPro 300.0 battery. Working in concert, the battery and saw deliver power at a constant level, maintaining a chain speed through most of the saw’s runtime.

Aside from run-time and consistent power delivery, the MSA 260 C-O has the ability to chew through big, tough logs. Note that Stihl positions the MSA 260 C-O “for professional applications” so the performance does come at a cost, $770 for the 18-inch model we tested, plus another $350 for the battery. While that might seem like a lot, it’s in line with other pro battery and gas saws. So, if you’re a frequent chainsaw user, the investment is going to be worth it.

Husqvarna may not have been the fastest-cutting electric chainsaw we tested, but it’s at the top of our list because it does just about everything well—it operates smoothly, cuts fast (enough), and is easy to use. With the boost button engaged, it blazed through our cutting tests, slicing through an 8-inch log in about 5 seconds. It also had the fastest windup speed of any electric chainsaw that I personally tested—it was ready to cut as soon as I pulled the trigger. Even with its impressive power, this chainsaw is surprisingly quiet, producing 67 decibels at 60 feet. The Power Axe isn’t particularly light at 14.5 pounds with battery, but it’s well balanced. I had no problem holding it steady even when making cuts at waist-level height.

The Power Axe also comes with a suite of well-designed, user-friendly features. The power button and boost button are located just above the handle so they’re easier to access on the fly. You also have the ability to adjust the chain tension by hand, loosening a dial to release the tension, then rotating a dial to tighten it back up. The whole process takes seconds. There’s also a built-in chain brake to protect from injuries. It’s a well-built, high-performing electric chainsaw that still manages to come in at a reasonable price.

Echo’s DCS-5000 gives you what you want most out of a chainsaw: raw cutting power. It may lack some of the bells and whistles you’ll find on pricier models, such as tool-less chain tensioning and power-saving settings, but its base performance is right up there with the Power Axe 350i. The DCS-5000 cut through 8-inch hardwood in less than 4 seconds. That's saying something considering that the DCS-5000 costs at least $100 less than any other saw that can do the same.

Along with its impressive cutting speed, the Echo is also relatively light at just over 12 pounds. I could feel the difference whenever I had to lift it up above shoulder-height to cut higher limbs, or while crouching down to cut a fallen 8-inch beech tree.

There is a trade-off with the DCS-5000, though, in that it lacks any kind of quality-of-life-related features. You won’t find tool-less chain tensioning or ECO settings that conserve battery life here. It’s pretty loud, too, producing 76 decibels at 60 feet. And, finally, while I appreciated the lighter weight, I could also feel the vibrations in my arm more than electric chainsaws with more refined builds.

Still, if you don't mind the rougher feel, higher noise level, and lack of a few user-friendly features you’ll find on pricier electric chainsaws, the Echo DCS-5000 offers exceptional value.

The newest saw from Makita, the 40V Max XGT 20-inch chainsaw, is a beast. The brand claims it offers the power of a 50-cc gas saw, and we’re inclined to agree. The first pull of the trigger surprised us with the speed and sound of the chain whizzing around. And it wasn’t just the noise that was impressive—in testing, it positively tore through logs.

Using the 40V Max XGT for practical tasks like felling trees, or cleaning up fallen trees after a storm, confirmed its power. I tackled logs as big as 18 inches, and the saw left nothing to be desired—it handled everything I used it on, without faltering. The 40V Max XGT chainsaw, at 20-inches, is among the biggest battery powered chainsaws we test. Our test unit came as a kit, with everything you need, including an 8.0Ah battery and charger. The kit isn’t cheap, but if you’re already on the Makita 40-volt XGT platform, you can save $500 and buy the chainsaw as a tool only.

The Stihl MSA 80 is an easy-to-use chainsaw that also packs impressive cutting power. Its compact build—the 14-inch saw weighs about 10 pounds—gives it a balanced feel in hand. It features a narrow bar that makes it easier to fit it into the crotches of trees for pruning than other electric chainsaws we tested like the Ego 20-inch chainsaw or Husqvarna Power Axe 350i. Likewise, it comes equipped with a thinner chain that cuts more smoothly through wood, producing less vibration. Lastly, its variable speed trigger allows you to adjust the speed of the blade to suit the job at hand.

There are several user-friendly features worth noting. Chain adjustments are effortless: Simply loosen a knob, turn the adjuster, and tighten the knob. There’s also a lubrication system to keep the chain moving with a minimal amount of bar oil.

Stihl’s chainsaws come at a premium price, and this one is no different. At $500, the MSA 80 is the most expensive electric chainsaw we tested, despite having a relatively short bar. Even so, it’s an ideal choice for homeowners who are looking for professional-grade build quality and performance out of an electric chainsaw.

If you already have other Echo tools, like a leaf blower or trimmer, then you can save a little cash and purchase the DCS-2500T as a tool only.

There’s a price for the CS-2005’s cutting power and capacity, though—its weight. While we liked its buttery smooth operation, it weighs an arm-burning 19.3 pounds with a battery. Making cuts on limbs above the head or below the knees was a far more challenging experience than with electric chainsaws we tested that were 4 to 5 pounds lighter. The Ego CS-2005 also lacks a tool-free chain tensioning system. If you don’t mind the extra heft, the Ego CS-2005 is the high-powered electric you’ll want for really big jobs.

As new models come out, we can’t keep all the models we test on our recommended list. But some of these older models and runners-up are still worth considering. Here are a few:

Milwaukee M18 Fuel 18V 16-Inch Electric Chainsaw: Don’t be fooled by the Milwaukee’s lower voltage, this saw has plenty of power for quickly cutting up large limbs or felling medium-sized trees. It’s a good saw, but we’d mostly recommend it to folks already on Milwaukee’s M18 platform, so you can buy it as a tool only and save some money.

Stihl GTA 26: While folks often refer to tools like this as “mini chain saws”, Stihl bills the GTA 26 as a garden pruner. Intended specifically for pruning shrubs and small branches, the GTA 26 combines light weight and a pistol-like configuration to make an easy-to-handle tool. Think of it as a power loppers, with greater size capability.

Worx Nitro WG385 40V 16-Inch Chainsaw: At less than $300, the Worx Nitro WG385 can get the job done. It’s lighter and easier to maneuver than other similarly sized saws, but it’s also a little less powerful, with a little less run-time. Still, with some patience we were able to find it useful for basic property maintenance. For the price, the Worx Nitro WG385 is a good cost-effective option for occasional use.

At the heart of an electric chainsaw is its motor and battery, which need to work in concert to perform well. The longer the chainsaw bar, the bigger the log or tree you can cut. So, the motor has to have enough torque and speed, while the battery has to be able to deliver the power needed to sustain that torque and speed. This is one reason why bigger saws can cost a lot more than smaller ones. Batteries are a significant factor in the price of a saw, and bigger batteries cost more. Battery size is typically measured in amp-hours (Ah), and larger saws will typically have 8 to 12 Ah batteries.

Mini chainsaws, made for pruning and other small jobs, feature a one-handed design with a small cutting bar—4 to 8 inches—and a small battery. If you need to break down large branches from trees, opt for a smaller full-size chainsaw with a 12- to 14-inch cutting bar. For something professional-grade, consider a large chainsaw with a 16- to 20-inch cutting bar and larger battery.

Among electric chainsaws with comparable bar lengths, those with larger batteries will typically have more ripping power, which means they’ll cut through limbs and logs more quickly, allowing you to get the job done faster. Just keep in mind that a more powerful chainsaw is also often a heavier chainsaw.

An electric chainsaw’s runtime depends on the capacity of its battery, in amp-hours. Broadly speaking, more capacity means more juice, but larger saws draw more power per second, so it’s not uncommon to find large chainsaws with large batteries getting comparable battery life to their smaller, more efficient counterparts.

Since your chainsaw only uses power when you’re using it to cut, its true runtime comes down to how many cuts it can make on a single charge. The Popular Mechanics test team tests runtime by checking how many times each model cuts through a 6-inch log on a single charge. Depending on battery size and chainsaw power, that number can vary quite a bit—from as low as 50 to as high as 300.

The “cutting bar” is the business end of the chainsaw—the long, rounded metal guide that the chain spins around to create a blade. The length of the bar, which is measured from the base of the chainsaw’s head to the tip of the cutting bar, determines the maximum thickness of the wood that your chainsaw can cut through in a single pass. Generally speaking, your chainsaw can safely cut through limbs with a diameter up to 2 inches less than the length of the bar. For example, a 12-inch chainsaw can cut through a limb up to 10 inches in diameter in a single pass.

For a long time, going electric meant settling for a chainsaw with a shorter bar, but that’s no longer the case. While gas engines offer more power, especially in larger saws, there are electric options with professional-length cutting bars. For instance, a 20-inch electric chainsaw has the power to bring down mature trees.

Need is an important distinction here. We don’t recommend picking the most powerful saw with the longest cutting bar within your budget. A longer bar will give you more cutting capacity, but it’s also heavier and more difficult to handle safely. We recommend buying the tool that’s just big enough for the cutting tasks you’ll perform regularly. If you’re looking to trim small limbs and branches, a pruning saw with a 4- to 8-inch bar such as the Stihl GTA 26 is going to be the best choice. If you’re taking down trees and breaking them down for firewood, you may need something longer.

It’s important that you feel comfortable handling the weight of your chainsaw. If it’s too heavy to hold at chest height, then you can’t use it safely.

With electric chainsaws, weight is directly related to the length of the cutting bar. The longer the cutting bar, the heavier the chainsaw. A mini chainsaw with an 8-inch bar will weigh around 5 pounds, while a large 18-inch chainsaw can weigh as much as 15 pounds.

One thing to keep in mind: Many manufacturers list the “bare” weight of their cordless chainsaws without the battery, so when you do add that in, it can mean an extra 1 to 3 pounds to the load.

Chainsaws are among the most dangerous power tools you can put your hands on: According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), some 36,000 people ends up in the hospital with chainsaw-related injuries each year.

That is why it’s crucial to always take every step you can to ensure the safest possible conditions when using a chainsaw. A good electric chainsaw should have built-in safety features to minimize the possibility of an accident: First, look for a trigger lock, which requires you to maintain pressure on a button near the handle to keep the chain spinning. Most will also have a chain brake at the front of the handle, which disengages the chain if the chainsaw kicks back.

That depends on the size of the tree. Even the largest electric chainsaws with a 20-inch cutting bar only have the torque necessary to cut down moderately tall trees. You’ll want a gas chainsaw that brings the extra power to bring down an older, larger tree, or if you’re bringing trees down regularly.

Electric chainsaws don’t require much maintenance. Use a brush or cloth to remove sawdust and other debris that’s caught in the chain and sprocket area after each use.

You should also clean any debris that’s fallen into the air intake slots: Brush it away material on the outside with a clean paintbrush, then blow the valves out with compressed air.

Lastly, electric chainsaws have an auto-lubrication mechanism, but we recommend checking the chain oil reservoir before each use, and refilling it if necessary.

Many cordless electric chainsaws are water resistant and will work fine in the rain. Before you take one for a spin, check its Ingress Protection rating (IP), which measures dust and waterproofing. If your chainsaw features an “IPX3” rating, it can handle “sprays of water” and should be fine in light rain. An “IPX4” rating can withstand moderate to heavy rain.

All that said, we don’t really recommend operating a chainsaw in the rain. Even with work gloves, rain makes every chainsaw-related job less safe, so hold off until the sun comes out if you can.

You should never use a corded electric chainsaw in the rain. Doing so presents a significant electrocution risk. (It’s one of many reasons we generally don’t recommend them.)

He’s tested and written about everything from home security systems to power tools to gas grills. Crock-Pot Chicken Noodle Soup

📅 2017-09-21  |  Read Source →

The pinnacle of cozy, comforting food is chicken noodle soup. When you need something warming and satisfying, there’s just nothing better. While our classic recipe is already easy to whip up, tossing all of your ingredients in the slow cooker makes this soup easier than ever. After a few hours and very little effort, you’ll have the coziest meal on your hands, ready to warm you up all fall and winter long.

What People Are Saying:“Perfect for snow days. Easy to make and there is no wrong time for soup” - evabnyc2372

“ Very easy....very good thank god I made a big pot bc I have 3 boys my oldest had 5 servings and my youngest had two giant servings my middle son had 3” - ashleybaby3586

The best thing about this recipe? It’s not necessary to cook the chicken before putting it in the soup—let your slow cooker do all the hard work for you. Place the boneless, skinless chicken breasts in the slow cooker, along with the veggies, herbs, butter, and broth. Season with salt and pepper, then cook on high with the lid closed for 2 1/2 hours.

By now, the chicken should be cooked and tender. Transfer it to a plate, and after letting it cool slightly, use 2 forks to shred it (or whatever is your favorite shredding method).

Discard the thyme, rosemary, and bay leaf from the slow cooker, then add the shredded chicken back in along with the egg noodles. Cover and cook on High for about 5 to 10 minutes—you're looking for the noodles to be al dente.

The full list of ingredients and instructions can be found in the recipe below.

If you have leftovers, I strongly suggest that you store the noodles separately from the broth. If they’re stored together, the noodles will soak up the liquid and turn to mush, and there won’t be any broth left. If you’d like to cook this soup in advance, the same recommendation applies, though if you’re making it ahead of time, I'd recommend skipping the noodles completely. You can add them to the broth when you reheat the soup, and they should be fully cooked in just 5 minutes.

Same deal if you're freezing—the soup itself freezes well and can be reheated whenever the craving hits, but the noodles will get mushy sitting in all of that broth, so I'd recommend cooking them off fresh!

Sweet Potato Casserole

📅 2017-09-18  |  Read Source →

Move aside, other casseroles—sweet potato casserole ranks at the very top of my list when it comes to Thanksgiving sides. I'll be coming back to this recipe time and time again, thanks to its buttery pecan topping. It’s still plenty sweet (thanks to the brown sugar), but not enough to make the sweet potatoes taste like straight-up dessert.

What People Are Saying:“I made this for a Thanksgiving carry in a couple of years ago which was a hit, and just put it in the oven for this years dinner that I’m hosting! Delicioso, my family love this version the best. Thank you!☺️” - dmartin0958214

After preheating the oven and greasing a baking dish with cooking spray, place your sweet potatoes in a large pot and cover with water. Bring them to a boil, then reduce the heat to medium-low and simmer until the potatoes are tender and you can poke them with a fork. Drain the water, then let them cool slightly before transferring to a large bowl. Add the butter to the potatoes, then mash with a potato masher or 2 forks.

Mash until you reach your desired consistency (I prefer it smooth with a few chunks). Once they’ve reached the consistency you’re looking for, add eggs, milk, brown sugar, salt, nutmeg, and vanilla and stir until smooth. Once mixed, transfer to the prepared baking dish.

In a medium bowl, mix all of the topping ingredients together until combined.

Once combined, sprinkle the topping over the sweet potato mixture. Bake the casserole until it is warmed through and the topping is golden brown, then serve immediately while warm.

The full list of ingredients & instructions can be found in the recipe below.

This is a great make-ahead Thanksgiving recipe to save yourself time (and energy) on the big day. Prep the casserole up to step 2, then refrigerate until you're ready to bake (we recommend no more than a few days ahead of time). When you're ready to bake, you can prepare and top with the crumbly topping.

If you have any leftovers (which I can't imagine you would), put them in an airtight container—or cover your casserole dish—and refrigerate for three to five days. This casserole also freezes wonderfully. Similar to prepping it ahead, simply prepare your casserole and bake it without topping it with the pecan topping. Allow it to cool before covering and freezing. When you're ready to serve, simply thaw it in the fridge overnight, top it with the crumbly topping, and bake.

13 Best Deodorant for Men 2026, According to Dermatologists

📅 2017-06-11  |  Read Source →

Frankly, the best deodorant for men sells itself. It stinks to stink: Body odor is right up there with bad breath as a totally avoidable misfortune. But while deodorant might seem like an odd category to get overly psyched about, as a longtime grooming editor, I can confidently say we're living in a golden age of the underarm essential.

Unlike the synthetic and chalky ghosts of deodorant sticks past, today’s best deodorants are a a sea of sophisticated formulas, improved textures, better scents, and naturally derived ingredients that, yes, actually work. The only hard part is honing in on the best one for you. Price, feel, skin sensitivity, and your sweat and odor levels matter. So I tested a whole lot of deodorants to narrow the field to seven standouts, some new, some tried-and-true, plus a few more deeper cuts I also loved.

I also tapped trusted dermatologists, for some expert insights further down. Just one last quick note: We’re not talking antiperspirants here, which primarily rely on aluminum compounds to reduce sweat. If that’s what you’re after, I’ve got recommendations for those, too.

Now, are there more targeted, more sophisticated, and, well, chicer deodorants on this list? Absolutely. But at the end of the day, you want your deo to do three things: keep the stink at bay, smell good, and be easy to repurchase. Dove Men+Care hits every mark, which is why it's in every drugstore.

The price, of course, is great, but then there's the scent: a surprisingly sophisticated blend of bright bergamot and warm white amber that's fresh, subtly citrusy, and softly warm. It smells so much better than your typical underarm fare and is actually a little mood-boosting, as the packaging suggests. The aluminum-free, baking-soda-free formula features moisture-attracting ingredients, giving it a little skincare credibility, too. It also feels super smooth going on, and kept its scent for most of the day. No notes!

Hip grooming brand Church California's deodorant only launched two years ago, but for me, it's everything a niche, forward-thinking deodorant should be: Effective, made with almost entirely naturally derived ingredients, and housed in compostable, plastic-free packaging. The formula uses magnesium and organic tapioca starch for odor and moisture control, with coconut oil and shea butter adding a little skin-conditioning goodness. And that scent? Wonderfully herbaceous, thanks to an essential oil blend that smells like the actual outdoors.

Admittedly, the sturdy cardboard tube takes a little getting used to—you push the stick up from the bottom instead of twisting a dial. And the glide is certainly a little less smooth. But oh, does the formula hold up. Even hours later, I'd catch little whiffs of those lovely botanicals, and when the scent eventually faded, there was still no detectable B.O. For the environmentally conscious guy, it's a no-brainer, and for the aesthete, it looks retro cool sitting on your medicine cabinet shelf.

Ursa Major’s Hoppin’ Fresh stick is a modern legend in the deo space. Among us grooming geeks, this au naturel-meets-elevated formula changed the underarm game when it launched in 2015. It was one of those early artisanal step-ups from drugstore fare, and it earned the hype with thoughtfully curated, foraged-sounding ingredients, a subtle, natural-smelling citrus scent, and a skincare-centric stick that glides on smoothly.

It still holds up today, fighting odor, soothing skin, and absorbing excess moisture to keep you smelling fresh. For an aluminum-free deo, it also keeps me pretty dry, too. And even the packaging—a sleek, solid-blue tube—feels like a style upgrade. Scent wise, in winter Forest Fix is the way to go. But the original Hoppin' Fresh is always waiting in the wings once the warmer months come around.

I'm well aware that Old Spice has plenty of newer, more fragrance-centric variations, many with names like Swagger. But I've always preferred the tried-and-true stuff, and that blue stick, here in High Endurance Refresh, is about as iconic as deodorant gets. It's my old reliable, and one I always keep as a backup, whether I'm visiting family, staying overnight somewhere, or just need something I can count on in a pinch. This classic has aged remarkably well, because it simply works.

Then there's the nostalgia, which hits me every time I catch a whiff of that distinctively fresh-meets-medicinal aroma I've loved unironically for years. The formula isn't particularly fancy: science-y ingredients give the aluminum-free stick its smooth glide, while glycerin adds a little skin-conditioning benefit. Would I wear this for an Ironman competition? Probably not. But for everyday errands, workdays, and just about everything in between? Absolutely. At under $15 for a three-pack, it's also easily one of the best value options on this list.

When you think gel deodorant, you probably picture those old-school perforated dispensers with tons of holes. But Baxter of California's is a smooth, transparent gel stick that's still, in my book, among the best. Part of the appeal is the formula, which packs some impressive botanicals: witch hazel and tea tree oil help tackle odor-causing bacteria, while chamomile helps soothe skin. Add tried-and-true glycerin to the mix, and you've got a gel deodorant with some serious skincare cred, too.

Then there's the application itself. The clear gel glides on effortlessly, with a cool, crisp, almost medicinal feel that's especially refreshing during the warmer months. Better still, it won't leave white marks on your shirts, something I always appreciate with my go-to black T-shirts. Is it the most heavy-duty odor fighter on this list? Not quite. But for everyday wear, it keeps the funk at bay without feeling like you've applied much of anything.

I test a lot of deodorants, but few become permanent fixtures in my bathroom. So it's worth telling you that Freaks of Nature's Everyday Endurance has become my primary deodorant lately. The brand may be known best for sunscreen, but it knows its way around our underarms too. And much like its sunscreens, part of the appeal is the application. The lotion-like formula emerges through a clever F-shaped grate, and there's something oddly satisfying about watching it dispense before smoothing it onto your underarms.

Better still, it absorbs well without any gunky, sticky residue. Cocoa butter and sunflower oil give it a skin-conditioning quality that makes this one of the most soothing deodorants I've ever worn. It's premium-priced, sure, but between the standout application, exemplary performance, and skin-conditioning feel, it's worth every penny.

Gone are the days when deodorant had to smell like “Sports Ice Rush.” Today's best odor fighters offer sophisticated, subtle scents (so they don't compete with your cologne), and Salt & Stone remains one of the top examples, with deodorants that have actual scent pyramids—Saffron & Cedar, Black Rose & Oud, Bergamot & Hinoki, for example. All are nuanced, refined, and never too loud.

But it's not just about the scent. The formula neutralizes odor and absorbs excess moisture very well, while skincare-centric additions like niacinamide and shea butter help keep underarms comfortable. More importantly, in my experience, the fragrance doesn't devolve into some funky olfactory murk by day's end. You still smell like the scent you applied that morning, or at the very least, fresh. Are these the heaviest-duty or most affordable deodorants out there? Nope. But they deliver on fragrance and function, and that's a combination worth investing in.

Caldera Lab just launched its first deodorant, and given the brand's skincare chops, it's no surprise the formula reads like a serum: niacinamide, aloe vera, and hyaluronic acid soothe and hydrate; postbiotics and magnesium tackle odor; and arrowroot absorbs moisture. Stacked, indeed.

Can Aesop do any wrong? Not really, and its deodorant spray is another example of the botanical-meets-aromatic goodness the amber-hued brand does so well. It's the only spray on this list, and while its odor protection holds up well, a midday refresh is sometimes warranted—and honestly, part of the pleasure.

If your skin is extra sensitive, Nécessaire's entire fragrance-free lineup is exemplary, and its deodorant stick is no exception. It keeps odor impressively in check, is easy on sensitive underarms, and won't compete with your favorite cologne. It's also aluminum- and baking soda-free, dermatologist-tested, hypoallergenic, and even carries the National Eczema Association's Seal of Acceptance.

Cremo may be kings of shave cream, but it knows a thing or two about deodorant, too. The formulas are affordable, the odor protection holds up, and the scents smell so damn good. Take Palo Santo, with its warm, woody, slightly smoky aroma that feels far more sophisticated than its price suggests. Coconut oil and shea butter add the skin-conditioning touch you'd expect from a pricier stick. rant quietly in the background, look elsewhere—but if you like a lot of fragrance with your funk-fighting, Cremo delivers serious bang for the buck.

The Grey's culty, rightfully hyped deodorant is the best old-school-style roll-on I've ever tried. The unscented formula is so lightweight you almost have to check you're applying anything, but oh, you are, and there's a cooling tingle as it goes on. It's the most expensive deodorant on this list, but with its premium slim aluminum casing and impressively lightweight formula, it's an investment in barely-there odor-fighting care.

Malin+Goetz's Eucalyptus Deodorant is a stone-cold classic that helped elevate gel sticks and make them feel refreshingly natural. The scent is practically a trademark at this point, equal parts medicinal, herbal, and oh-so-subtle. But there's substance behind it: eucalyptus extract helps refresh skin, while odor-neutralizing citronellyl methylcrotonate keeps the funk at bay.

As a grooming editor, I've tried more sticks, sprays, gels, and roll-ons than I can count, from drugstore staples to niche newcomers. And while plenty have impressed me on first swipe, the real test is whether I keep reaching for them weeks, months, or even years later.

Here, I drew on firsthand testing, focusing on odor protection, scent, texture, application, skin comfort, and, of course, how well they hold up. I've worn these deodorants through warm and cool weather, long days, and everything in between. Some are favorites I've relied on for years, while others are newer that are now part of my medicine cabinet rotation. As I've said, the best deodorant sometimes depends on the occasion or even the season, so it's a good idea to have a few on hand if possible. At the end of the day, a deodorant needs to keep B.O. at bay, ideally absorb excess moisture, and feel comfortable. Some excel in certain areas more than others, but each one above warrants its spot among the best deodorants.

Here is some dermatologist advice on deodorants—the best ingredients to look for, plus some controversial ones, too—with insights from board-certified dermatologists Kiran Mian, Ryan B. Turner, and Heather Rogers.

The primary cause of body odor is “bad” bacteria pairing up with sweat. There are two ways to prevent the stink: Reduce perspiration in the area, and/or create an inhospitable environment for the bacteria.

These are the most common ingredients to seek out if you want to temper the proliferation of bacteria in your pits (and anywhere you’re particularly pungent).

This antimicrobial and anti-inflammatory mineral safely and effectively kills bacteria without irritating skin, says Mian.

Dr. Mian also endorses the non-irritating magnesium hydroxide, a naturally occurring compound that neutralizes bacteria without absorbing into the skin.

“The terpenes in tea tree oil make this ingredient naturally antibacterial and antifungal, helping to reduce body odor,” Turner says. “In its pure, concentrated form, it can be very irritating. It’s best to use a product that’s formulated with tea tree oil at low percentages. It’s still effective when diluted, and a little bit goes a very long way.”

This one is controversial to some. Most skin types will tolerate baking soda (sodium bicarbonate) just fine, but only trust reputable, well-reviewed brands who use it. “Baking soda has a very alkaline pH level, while that of the skin is more acidic,” explains Turner. “This disrupts the skin’s natural barrier, harming the good bacteria in our microbiome, and leaving our skin more susceptible to irritation and inflammation.” (We’ve included a few deodorants with baking soda below, plus many natural picks that explicitly exclude it.)

It’s easy to get these two confused, but they share a principle. “Prebiotics feed the good bacteria on our skin (bacteria that does not break down sweat releasing a foils odor), which then overpopulates those odor-producing bacteria,” explains Mian. “Probiotics are the good bacteria that will ideally populate the skin and overtake odor causing bacteria.” The names of these might be strange to you, like lactobacillus acidophilus or saccharomyces ferment. Chances are, a brand will proudly tout their pre- and probiotics, so look for that indication instead.

“These chemical exfoliating ingredients help resurface the skin, which also strips away bacteria as well as removes the dead skin that bacteria break down, which causes body odor,” says Turner. And Dr. Rogers echoes the love: “Using acids to exfoliate the skin in our armpits helps rebuild the acidic mantle [which is compromised with age], leading to better control of bacterial growth and odor. Many acids work great; glycolic, lactic acid and mandelic acid are my favorites. Salicylic acid, a beta hydroxy acid, has a higher chance of irritation.”

“Activated charcoal can help to absorb sweat and help to neutralize odors,” says Turner.

Arrowroot powder, tapioca starch, and cornstarch are popular ingredients in aluminum-free deodorants, helping absorb excess moisture and keep underarms feeling dry without blocking sweat production. Of tapioca starch, Mian says, “This non-irritating starch won’t clog sweat ducts like aluminum, which can cause breakouts.” (It's also terrific in anti-chafe products for similar moisture-absorbing benefits.) “Like tapioca starch, corn starch pulls moisture from the skin to leave a powdery feeling behind. Unless you have an allergy to these plants, they are safer to use,” says Turner. Both doctors particularly favor arrowroot powder as a naturally derived, generally well-tolerated ingredient for absorbing moisture.

Both help soak up surface moisture without blocking sweat production. Freaks of Nature's formula includes bentonite clay.

A naturally occurring, porous mineral powder that absorbs excess moisture and helps keep underarms dry. You'll find it in Ursa Major's deodorant.

Approach your deodorant purchase with the following considerations:

While it’s easy to celebrate a fully natural formula—and for good reason—keep in mind that not all natural ingredients are inherently good for your skin. Ingredients like baking soda and certain essential oils can cause irritation, particularly if your underarms are sensitive. Conversely, synthetic ingredients aren't automatically bad for your skin, either. “What’s more important is to consider the manufacturer of the products—are they reputable? Do they perform extensive testing? What are the ingredients used and from where are they sourced?” says Turner. That's sound advice whether you're shopping natural or synthetic. There's a reason dermatologists frequently recommend established brands like Dove: They have the resources to develop, test, and manufacture formulas at scale, often at a fraction of the price of more niche alternatives. Of course, that doesn't mean smaller brands aren't doing their homework, either. Finding the right deodorant still takes some trial and error, gents, but you can always start with our list.

If your underarms are easily irritated, start with the ingredient list. Baking soda, strong fragrances, essential oils, and certain acids can trigger irritation, though everyone's skin reacts differently. Look for skin-conditioning ingredients like shea butter, glycerin, and aloe, several of which you'll find in the deodorants featured above. Fragrance-free formulas are a smart starting point for sensitive skin, while unscented are worth considering, too—just know that some contain masking fragrances.

Deodorants come in all sorts of formats these days, from classic solid sticks and clear gels to sprays, roll-ons, lotions, and pastes. How they feel when you apply them matters as much as how well they work. Some glide on more smoothly, while others feel chalkier, thicker, or more moisturizing. Gels and roll-ons tend to feel lightweight and refreshing (great for warmer weather), while richer lotions and pastes feel more nourishing and suit the cooler months. Ultimately, the best deodorant feels comfortable, doesn't leave residue on your clothes, and fits your lifestyle.

Dr. Kiran Mian is a board-certified dermatologist at Hudson Dermatology and Laser Surgery in NYC, and volunteer faculty at the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai University.

Dr. Ryan B. Turner is a board-certified dermatologist and founder of Turner Dermatology in NYC and the TRNR Skin skin care line.

Dr. Heather Rogers is a board-certified dermatologist and co-founder of Modern Dermatology in Seattle and the founder of the Doctor Rogers skin care line.

BA’s Best Green Bean Casserole With Step-by-Step Video

📅 2016-11-11  |  Read Source →

This green bean casserole recipe skips the canned soup. Chris Morocco’s take on the classic Thanksgiving side pairs fresh green beans and deeply browned crimini mushrooms with a garlicky Parmesan béchamel. As for the topping, French’s fried onions are iconic for good reason, and they are staying right where they belong.

Give the mushrooms room to brown, salt the blanching water generously (like you would for cooking pasta), and taste the mushrooms and sauce before assembling. As written, you can cook the mushrooms and make the sauce at the same time. To save a pan, cook the mushrooms first, then transfer them to a plate and make the béchamel in the same pan, scraping up all the browned bits.

For the rest of the spread, turn to our classic Thanksgiving menu →

Can I make green bean casserole ahead of time?

Yes. Blanch the green beans, cook the mushrooms, and make the sauce up to 1 day ahead; cool and refrigerate them separately. Assemble and bake the day you plan to serve it, adding the fried onions during the final few minutes of baking.

We don’t recommend freezing the assembled casserole—the cream sauce can separate, and the beans turn mushy after thawing and reheating.

How do I keep the cream sauce from separating?

Don’t overreduce it. Once the béchamel is thick and bubbling, take it off the heat before whisking in the garlic and Parmesan. Boiling off too much liquid can cause the fat to separate. Stick with the recipe’s amount of Parmesan, too—extra cheese can contribute to a broken sauce.

Blanched, fresh beans are our first choice, but readers have reported success with frozen petite and French-cut beans. We haven’t tested the swap, but recommend thawing them overnight in your refrigerator and straining the excess liquid before continuing.

Can I make this green bean casserole gluten-free?

We haven’t tested a gluten-free version, but our editors suggest replacing the all-purpose flour with a gluten-free flour blend. You’ll also need to seek out gluten-free fried onions, or try our flourless Crispy Fried Shallots.

Place rack in middle of oven; preheat oven to 375°. Working in batches, cook 2 lb. fresh green beans in a large pot of boiling salted water until bright green and halfway to tenderness, about 3 minutes per batch. Drain and let cool; trim stem ends.

Heat 1 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil in a large skillet over medium-high. Cook ½ lb. crimini mushrooms, sliced, without stirring, until golden brown underneath, about 2 minutes. Toss and continue to cook, tossing occasionally, until browned on both sides, about 3 minutes more. Add 2 Tbsp. unsalted butter and 2 large thyme sprigs to pan. Cook, tossing occasionally, until butter browns and mushrooms are dark brown and very tender, about 4 minutes longer. Season with kosher salt and transfer to a plate. Repeat with 2 Tbsp. unsalted butter, salt, and remaining 1 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil, ½ lb. crimini mushrooms, sliced, and 2 large thyme sprigs.

Melt remaining 2 Tbsp. unsalted butter in a large saucepan over medium-low. Whisk in 2 Tbsp. all-purpose flour and cook, whisking occasionally, until roux is golden brown and smells mildly nutty, about 4 minutes. Whisk in 1¼ cups whole milk and 1 cup heavy cream. Increase heat and bring to a simmer, whisking often, and cook until béchamel sauce is thick and bubbling, about 5 minutes. Remove from heat and whisk in 4 garlic cloves, finely grated, and ½ cup grated Parmesan; season with salt and freshly ground pepper.

Arrange green beans and mushrooms in a 2-qt. baking dish. Pour sauce over bean mixture (it may not look like enough, but it will get saucier as it bakes). Cover tightly with foil and bake until sauce is bubbling, 25–30 minutes. Uncover and continue to bake until casserole is lightly browned on top and around the edges, 15–20 minutes longer. Top with ¾ cup French’s fried onions and continue to bake just until onions are slightly darker and fragrant, about 3 minutes. Let sit 10 minutes to set before serving.

Editor’s note: This green bean casserole recipe first appeared in November 2016 as part of BA’s Best, our vault of the Test Kitchen’s definitive takes on iconic dishes. Head this way for more of our go-to Thanksgiving recipes →

31 Thanksgiving Desserts to Cap Your Holiday Feast

📅 2016-08-15  |  Read Source →

There’s Thanksgiving dinner, and then there are Thanksgiving desserts. These are two separate events, and in between, it’s perfectly fine to take a disco nap. (In fact, we recommend it.)

“This is the magnum opus of Thanksgiving dessert recipes,” writes one reader whose 90-year-old grandfather asked for a repeat performance. “You bet your boots I will—it’s the best apple pie, ever.”

A colossal, self-serve mousse is a baller host move—no tidy slicing, no fussy garnishing, no jockeying for oven space. Set out bowls and spoons and let guests help themselves. Vanilla lovers, try our Colossal Crème Brûlée.

Butter-toasted pecans get the glory, but sour cream does the work, balancing both the gooey pecan layer and the pile of whipped cream. Between them? A thick layer of silky brown sugar custard.

A slice of Thanksgiving pie is nice, but how about a slice of pumpkin flan under a veneer of caramel? This one takes a page from Puerto Rican flan de queso, with cream cheese lending richness, tang, and luxurious texture.

Consider this pie a tangy exclamation point to the holiday meal: cranberry-lime curd in a flaky cream cheese crust, crowned with billows of meringue.

Brown sugar takes some of the fuss out of caramel cake’s famously finicky frosting while browned butter adds toasty depth. Spread it over tender yellow cake layers and finish with flaky salt.

Silky filling, flaky crust, a whisper of spice: This is pumpkin pie done right. Sweetened condensed milk gives the custard its richness; maple syrup and vanilla round out the flavor.

Skip the rolling pin: Buttered phyllo encloses an orange-scented custard, with crumpled sheets forming a dramatically ruffled top. Cinnamon sugar adds crunch to this no-roll pie.

This plush pumpkin spice cake wears a thick swoop of tangy cream cheese frosting. Bake the cake a day ahead, then add the frosting in the lull between dinner and dessert.

If most holiday desserts feel a tad too sweet for you, herbal rosemary and fruity olive oil give this cake just enough savory elements to justify a second helping.

Blanketing quartered apples with rough-puff pastry feels rustic enough, but once baked and inverted, this shimmering, blushing tart dominates the holiday table.

Sweet potato, warming spices, and orange zest get a gloss of dark chocolate in this autumnal loaf cake. Bake it up to three days ahead, leaving the ganache for later.

A deeply browned top and cracked sides are the goal here, not a baking mishap. Underneath: a custardy, pumpkin-spiced cheesecake that needs neither a crust nor a water bath.

This glamorous layer cake, with jewel-toned swipes of cranberry jam, white chocolate–cream cheese frosting, and sugared cranberries, looks and tastes like winter is coming (in the best possible way).

A no-bake Oreo crust, a rich chocolate filling, and a tower of whipped cream come together in this shut-the-room-up dessert.

Caramel-glazed apple halves crown this nutty upside-down cake, all baked in the same skillet used to brown the fruit. A scoop of vanilla ice cream on top? We wouldn’t say no.

Inspired by the flavors of a pumpkin spice latte, this rendition of old-school tiramisu calls for soaking ladyfingers in bourbon-spiked espresso, then enveloping them in plumes of pumpkin-mascarpone cream.

Pumpkin crumb cake is like the brownie of Thanksgiving desserts: easy to tote and slice for sharing. Here, sweet rice flour gives the cake a chewy bounce beneath a buttery, crispy-crumbly pecan streusel.

The ultraflaky crust barely contains the lofty, fudgy almond filling, but have faith: It will puff and dome as it bakes, then settle back into sliceable form once cool.

Alternate layers of pumpkin and chocolate batter create this Bundt cake’s mesmerizing marbling—no knife-swirling necessary. A blanket of chocolate ganache makes it equally appealing to the pumpkin crowd and the chocolate diehards.

The Rock of London: Looking back at John Terry’s Chelsea FC career

📅 2016-05-17  |  Read Source →

Home » Home 2 » The Rock of London: Looking back at John Terry’s Chelsea FC career

At the start of this season, John Terry leaving Chelsea was the last thing on anyone’s mind. All was rosy at Stamford Bridge, the home of the newly crowed English champions. In over 21 years at the club, there was no indication that Terry would not retire a Chelsea man.

The Blues’ skipper’s announcement in January 2016, then, came as a shock to the Chelsea faithful, bringing notice to the seemingly premature — even if inevitable — fall of the curtain on a legendary career.

After making his debut in the League Cup against Aston Villa late in 1998, John Terry made sporadic appearances under Gianluca Vialli, before joining Nottingham Forest on a short loan spell. He broke into the first team in 2000-01, when manager Claudio Ranieri handed him 22 Premier League appearances during a season that the 20-year-old would finish as the club’s Player of the Year.

It would be the first of several achievements to come.

Terry’s importance to the team grew as Ranieri relied on his partnership with then-captain Marcel Desailly to mastermind Chelsea’s progression from a fashionable mid-table club to a Champions League team that meant business. They received a major shot in the arm when Russian billionaire Roman Abramovich bought the club in 2003. It was also a significant year in Terry’s international career as he made his England debut against Serbia and Montenegro in June.

The season that followed saw Chelsea finish above perennial winners Manchester United for the first time in Premier League history, and defeat Premiership invincibles Arsenal en route to the club’s first Champions League semifinal. Terry ended the season making a first of four appearances in the PFA Team of the Year, and went to Portugal for the European Championships having displaced Sol Campbell as England’s first choice centre-back.

In the summer of 2004, Chelsea brought in Jose Mourinho, who was fresh from winning a treble with the unfancied FC Porto and raring to take on bigger challenges. The 23-year-old Terry was named captain as Chelsea embarked on the most successful period in the club’s history. In the three seasons that followed, Chelsea won 2 league cups, 2 Premier League titles and the FA Cup — success built on a core of John Terry, Frank Lampard, Petr Cech and Didier Drogba, Chelsea’s ‘old guard’.

This was also a period when Terry picked up several individual accolades. He was named PFA Player of the Year in 2005, the first defender to win the award since Paul McGrath in 1993. He would make a first of five consecutive appearances in the FIFPro World XI in 2005, as well as a first of four appearances in the UEFA Team of the Year.

He was also voted Chelsea’s Player of the Year for the second time in his career in 2006. Steve McClaren, hired as England manager after an underwhelming 2006 World Cup, named the Chelsea skipper as England captain.

Terry was now, beyond argument, one of football’s best.

However, more testing times were to come.

September 2007 saw the departure of Mourinho after a falling out with the club board. The season itself promised much but left a Chelsea squad dejected on three occasions as they saw the League Cup, the Premier League and the Champions League trophies go elsewhere.

For the European Cup in particular, Terry was left with no one but himself to blame as he slipped for the crucial penalty, a moment he has said will haunt him forever. Chelsea and Terry experienced further European heartbreak the following season as Tom Henning Ovrebo had a refereeing performance to forget at Stamford Bridge, handing Barcelona a berth in the final and eventually the trophy.

In early 2010, when he was revealed as the English footballer who had an affair with Vanessa Perroncel, Wayne Bridge’s ex-girlfriend — a horrendous personal error that cost him his status as England captain — it made him the perennial recipient of passionate booing across English grounds. After the revelation, Chelsea travelled to Burnley. Amidst a frosty reception, he scored a late winner to put Carlo Ancelotti’s side back on track in the title race, one they would go on to win in some style. All of this typified John Terry — imperfect, but unshakeable.

Terry had built an unflappable reputation as a top class defender, leader and hard man. He said about himself, “I still say if the ball is there to be won I will go for it, whether with my head or whatever, and if it means us scoring or stopping a goal, I won’t think twice.”

The period that followed, however, was not good for Chelsea or their captain. The team finished the 2010-11 season trophyless, and Carlo Ancelotti paid for it with his job. This was the first of two times in Terry’s career where he and his club would have an extremely underwhelming season following a dominant one, and it also marked the first attempt to phase him out of the first team.

New manager Andre Villas-Boas attempted to introduce a high defensive line that left Terry exposed on a number of occasions, and saw the young Portuguese manager fall back on quicker defenders to implement his system. He was also determined to make lesser use of Frank Lampard and displace Didier Drogba with Fernando Torres. These were unpopular moves that led to poor results and eventually his sacking. Terry was not helped by another personal abberation, mouthing racial profanity at Anton Ferdinand of QPR, an act that saw him land a lengthy ban and damage his position in the England squad almost beyond repair.

Interim manager Roberto Di Matteo reinstated the old guard, and was rewarded with the ultimate prize — the first Champions League in Chelsea’s history.

Terry retired from international football following EURO 2012, leaving him to focus on his club career. However, further rotation was to follow during the reign of Rafael Benitez, who played Terry only in games of lesser importance. This was something he could do with relative impunity owing to the fact that his stock could not have fallen any further among Chelsea fans.

The writing was seemingly on the wall, however. Unsavoury incidents were fresher in the memory than individual accolades, the skipper was out of favour for two seasons running, and was well over 30 years of age. Was John Terry done?

Mourinho did not share the views of Benitez and Villas-Boas. Where they saw a slowing, easily-exposed has-been, he saw an experienced warrior with an excellent reading of the game who could form a title-winning partnership with the quicker Gary Cahill.

Terry grabbed the opportunity he was handed with gusto, performing with excellence in Mourinho’s lower defensive block. The English pair formed the core of a defence that would go on to win the club’s fourth Premier League title in 2015, leading the table from the opening day to the end. It was a landmark achievement for Terry in particular as he played every minute of a league campaign for the first time in his career, aged 34.

Once again they tried to put him down. Once again he fed off it and rose tall.

Chelsea did not start the defence of their title well at all, gathering just 4 points from the first 4 matches. Mourinho, who asked Terry to take the club on his shoulders in 2004 and stood by him for two seasons in his second stint, hauled off his skipper at half-time at the Etihad in the second game of the season, and began to rotate him out of the team, giving an increasing number of starts to the young Kurt Zouma.

The season did not take much of a turn for the better even under interim manager Guus Hiddink, particularly in a defensive sense as the Blues continued to let in goals during a meek climb to the top half of the table, with or without Terry in the side.

A sending off at the Stadium of Light following an injury layoff is not the kind of ending that the fans desired for their beloved captain, who has emerged from the dugout 703 times in Chelsea colours and has been the face of the club through the most successful period in their history.

Offered a new deal by the club last week, the skipper responded, “The contract extension the club has offered me is a different role and I hope everyone will understand I want to take the time to consider it carefully before making a decision.” While fans will hope — for reasons emotional as well as those to do with football — that Terry stays on, the chances look slim.

Should the captain leave the ship, the Blues supporters’ emotions are best summed up by a certain banner that embellishes the Shed End: Captain, Leader, Legend.

Nothing but victory should suffice for Chelsea but with the way things have gone this season, it wouldn’t be too surprising if Villa came away with something from this tie.
